**Sabah** (Malay pronunciation: [\[ˈsabah\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Malay)) is a [state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_and_federal_territories_of_Malaysia) of [Malaysia](3607937.md) located in northern [Borneo](4518.md), in the region of [East Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Malaysia). Sabah has land borders with the Malaysian state of [Sarawak](28258.md) to the southwest and [Indonesia](14579.md)'s [North Kalimantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Kalimantan) province to the south. The [Federal Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Territory_\(Malaysia\)) of [Labuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labuan) is an island just off Sabah's west coast. Sabah shares maritime borders with [Vietnam](202354.md) to the west and the [Philippines](23440.md) to the north and east. [Kota Kinabalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kota_Kinabalu) is the state capital and the economic centre of the state, and the seat of the [Sabah State government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Sabah). Other major towns in Sabah include [Sandakan](1732138.md) and [Tawau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawau). The 2020 census recorded a population of 3,418,785 in the state.[^cite_note-Malaysia_census_2020-9] It has an [equatorial climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equatorial_climate) with tropical [rainforests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainforest), abundant with animal and plant species. The state has long [mountain ranges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_range) on the west side which forms part of the [Crocker Range National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocker_Range_National_Park). [Kinabatangan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinabatangan_River), the second longest river in Malaysia runs through Sabah. The highest point of Sabah is [Mount Kinabalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Kinabalu), which is also the highest point of Malaysia.

The earliest human settlement in Sabah can be traced back to 20,000–30,000 years ago along the [Darvel Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darvel_Bay) area at the Madai-Baturong caves. The state has had a trading relationship with China starting from the 14th century AD. Sabah came under the influence of the [Bruneian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruneian_Empire) in the 14th and 15th centuries. The state was subsequently acquired by the British [North Borneo Chartered Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Borneo_Chartered_Company) in the 19th century. During [World War II](32927.md), North Borneo was [occupied by the Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_British_Borneo) for three years. It became a [British Crown Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Colony_of_North_Borneo) in 1946. On 31 August 1963, the newly-renamed Sabah was granted self-governance by the British. Following this, Sabah became one of the founding members of the Federation of Malaysia (established on 16 September 1963) alongside the [Crown Colony of Sarawak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Colony_of_Sarawak), the [Colony of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Singapore) (expelled in 1965), and the [Federation of Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Malaya) ([Peninsular Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_Malaysia) or West Malaysia). The federation was opposed by neighbouring Indonesia, which led to the [Indonesia–Malaysia confrontation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konfrontasi) over three years along with the threats of annexation by the Philippines along with the [Sultanate of Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu), threats which continue to the present day.[^cite_note-11]

Sabah has a diversity in ethnicity, culture and language. The head of state is the governor, also known as the [Yang di-Pertua Negeri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yang_di-Pertua_Negeri_Sabah), while the head of government is the [chief minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Minister_of_Sabah) and [his Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Sabah). The government system is closely modelled on the [Westminster parliamentary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_system) and has one of the earliest state legislature systems in Malaysia. Sabah is divided into five administrative divisions and 27 districts. [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_language) is the official language of the state;[^cite_note-official_language-12][^cite_note-English_restriction-13] and [Islam](6037917.md) is the state religion, but other religions may be practised.[^cite_note-state_religion-14] Sabah is known for its traditional musical instrument, the [sompoton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sompoton). Sabah has abundant natural resources, and its economy is strongly [export-oriented](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Export-oriented_economy). Its primary exports include oil, gas, timber and [palm oil](57561.md). The other major industries are agriculture and [ecotourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecotourism).

## Etymology

The origin of the name *Sabah* is uncertain, and many theories have arisen.[^cite_note-sabah_history-15] One theory is that when it was part of the [Bruneian Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruneian_Sultanate_\(1368%E2%80%931888\)), it was referred to as *Saba* because of the presence a variety of banana called *pisang saba* (also known as *pisang menurun*),[^cite_note-Sabah_origin-16][^cite_note-etymology-17] which are grown widely on the coast of the region and popular in [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei).[^cite_note-Hanum1989-18] The [Bajau community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bajau_people) referred to it as *pisang jaba*.[^cite_note-Hanum1989-18]

Additionally, during the Majapahit era, when Brunei was a [vassal state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassal_state) of [Majapahit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit), the [Old Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Javanese) eulogy of [Nagarakretagama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagarakretagama) described the area in what is now Sabah as *Seludang*.[^cite_note-Group-19][^cite_note-Sabah_origin-16]

Although the Chinese since the [Han dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_dynasty) had been associated with the island of [Borneo](4518.md),[^cite_note-ancient_china-20][^cite_note-21] they did not have any specific names for the area. Instead during the [Song dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_dynasty), they referred to the whole island as *Po Ni* (also pronounced *Bo Ni*), which is the same name they used to refer to the Sultanate of Brunei at the time.[^cite_note-sabah_history-15] Due to the location of Sabah in relation to Brunei, it has been suggested that *Sabah* was a [Brunei Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei_Malay) word meaning upstream or "in a northerly direction".[^cite_note-etymology-17][^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23] Another theory suggests that it came from the [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language) word *sabak* which means a place where [palm sugar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm_sugar) is extracted.[^cite_note-century-24]

It is nicknamed "Land Below the Wind" (Negeri Di Bawah Bayu in [Malay language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language)) as the state lies below the [typhoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon) belt of [East Asia](19605700.md) and is not often hit by typhoons.[^cite_note-land_below_the_wind-25][^cite_note-Welman2017-26][^cite_note-27]

## History

Main article: [History of Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Sabah)

### Prehistory

Main article: [Prehistoric Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Malaysia)



Entrance to the [Madai Cave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madai_Cave)

The earliest known human settlement in the region existed 20,000–30,000 years ago, as evidenced by stone tools and food remains found by excavations along the [Darvel Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darvel_Bay) area at Madai-Baturong caves near the Tingkayu River.[^cite_note-MacKinnon1996-28] The earliest inhabitants in the area were thought to be similar to [Aboriginal Australians](2912594.md), but the reason for their disappearance is unknown.[^cite_note-sabah_info-29] In 2003, archaeologists discovered the Mansuli valley in the [Lahad Datu District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahad_Datu_District), which dates back 235,000 years.[^cite_note-30] The archaeological site at [Skull Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skull_Hill,_Malaysia) (*Bukit Tengkorak*) in [Semporna District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semporna_District) was the largest pottery making site in [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32]

### Sultanates of Brunei and Sulu influences

During the 7th century AD, a settled community known as Vijayapura, a tributary to the [Srivijaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srivijaya) empire, was thought to have existed in northwest Borneo.[^cite_note-Hutton2000-33][^cite_note-Bala2005-34] The earliest independent kingdom in Borneo, supposed to have existed from the 9th century, was Po Ni, as recorded in the Chinese geographical treatise *[Taiping Huanyu Ji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiping_Huanyu_Ji)*. It was believed that Po Ni existed at the mouth of [Brunei River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei_River) and was the predecessor to the Bruneian Empire.[^cite_note-Bala2005-34][^cite_note-35] When China was conquered by the [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire), all Chinese vassal states were subsequently controlled by the Mongol emperors of China. Early in 1292, [Kublai Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kublai_Khan) is said to have sent an expedition to northern Borneo,[^cite_note-Runciman2011-36] before departing for the [invasion of Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Java) in 1293.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38] As a result of this campaign, it is believed that many of his followers in addition to other Chinese traders eventually settled and established their own enclave at [Kinabatangan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinabatangan_River).[^cite_note-Runciman2011-36]

In the 14th century, Brunei and Sulu were part of the Majapahit Empire but in 1369, Sulu and the other Philippine kingdoms successfully rebelled and Sulu even attacked Brunei which was still a Majapahit [tributary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tributary_state),[^cite_note-39] the Sulus specifically invaded Northeast Borneo at Sabah[^cite_note-40] the Sulus were then repelled but Brunei became weakened.[^cite_note-41] In 1370, Brunei transferred its allegiance to [Ming dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming_dynasty) China.[^cite_note-Chinese_records-42][^cite_note-Vienne2015-43] The [Maharaja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maharaja) Karna of Borneo then paid a visit to [Nanjing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanjing) with his family until his death.[^cite_note-44] He was succeeded by his son Hsia-wang who agreed to send tribute to China once every three years.[^cite_note-Chinese_records-42][^cite_note-Vienne2015-43] After that, Chinese [junks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junk_\(ship\)) came to northern Borneo with cargoes of spices, [bird nests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird_nest), shark fins, [camphor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camphor), [rattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rattan) and [pearls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearl).[^cite_note-Wise1846-45] More Chinese traders eventually settled in Kinabatangan, as stated in both Brunei and Sulu records.[^cite_note-Chinese_records-42][^cite_note-sabah_edu-46] A younger sister of [Ong Sum Ping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ong_Sum_Ping) (Huang Senping), the governor of the Chinese settlement then married [Sultan Ahmad of Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_Ahmad_of_Brunei).[^cite_note-Chinese_records-42][^cite_note-47] Perhaps due to this relationship, a burial place with 2,000 wooden coffins, some estimated to be 1,000 years old, were discovered in [Agop Batu Tulug Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agop_Batu_Tulug_Caves) and around the Kinabatangan Valley area.[^cite_note-coffins-48][^cite_note-49] It is believed that this type of funeral culture was brought by traders from [Mainland China](55079.md) and [Indochina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indochina) to northern Borneo as similar wooden coffins were also discovered in these countries.[^cite_note-coffins-48] This was in addition to the discovery of [Chinese ceramics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_ceramics) from a shipwreck at the [Tip of Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tip_of_Borneo) estimated to be from between 960 and 1127 AD from the Song dynasty and the Vietnamese [Đông Sơn drum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%90%C3%B4ng_S%C6%A1n_drum) in Bukit Timbang Dayang on [Banggi Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banggi_Island) that was between 2,000 and 2,500 years old.[^cite_note-sabah_info-29][^cite_note-50][^cite_note-Ooi2015-51]

During the reign of Sultan [Bolkiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolkiah) of Brunei between 1485 and 1524, the sultanate extended over northern Borneo and the [Sulu Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulu_Archipelago), as far as *[Kota Seludong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maynila_\(historical_entity\))* (present-day [Manila](184334.md)) with its [influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphere_of_influence) extending as far of [Banjarmasin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banjarmasin),[^cite_note-52] taking advantage of maritime trade after the [fall of Malacca to the Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_Malacca_\(1511\)).[^cite_note-HoltLambton1977-53][^cite_note-54] Many [Brunei Malays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruneian_Malay_people) migrated to Sabah during this period, beginning after the Bruneian conquest of the territory in the 15th century.[^cite_note-Brunei_migration-55] But plagued by internal strife, civil war, piracy and the [arrival of western powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_imperialism_in_Asia), the Bruneian Empire began to shrink. The first Europeans to visit Brunei were the Portuguese, who described the capital of Brunei at the time as surrounded by a [stone wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_wall).[^cite_note-HoltLambton1977-53] The Spanish followed, arriving soon after [Ferdinand Magellan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Magellan)'s death in 1521, when the remaining members of his expedition sailed to the islands of Balambangan and Banggi in the northern tip Borneo; later, in the [Castilian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castilian_War) of 1578, the Spanish who had sailed from [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain) and had taken Manila from Brunei, unsuccessfully declared war on Brunei by briefly occupying the capital before abandoning it.[^cite_note-Group-19][^cite_note-Ooi2015-51][^cite_note-Sidhu2009-56] The Sulu region gained its independence in 1578, forming the [Sultanate of Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu).[^cite_note-57]

When the [civil war broke out in Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruneian_Civil_War_of_1660) between sultans [Abdul Hakkul Mubin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hakkul_Mubin) and [Muhyiddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhyiddin_of_Brunei), the Sultan of Sulu asserted their claim to Brunei's territories in northern Borneo.[^cite_note-Sidhu2009-56][^cite_note-myph-58] The Sulus claimed that Sultan Muhyiddin had promised to cede the northern and eastern portion of Borneo to them in compensation for their help in settling the civil war.[^cite_note-Sidhu2009-56][^cite_note-Brunei_history-59] The territory seems have not been ceded formally, but the Sulus continued to claim the territory, with Brunei weakened and unable to resist.[^cite_note-ssc-60] After the war with the Spanish, the area in northern Borneo began to fall under the influence of the Sulu Sultanate.[^cite_note-Sidhu2009-56][^cite_note-Brunei_history-59] The [seafaring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seafarers) [Bajau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bajau_people)-[Suluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taus%C5%ABg_people) and [Illanun people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illanun_people) then arrived from the Sulu Archipelago and started settling on the coasts of north and eastern Borneo,[^cite_note-61] many of them fleeing from the oppression of [Spanish colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_East_Indies).[^cite_note-Indonesian_demographic-62] While the thalassocratic Brunei and Sulu sultanates controlled the western and eastern coasts of Sabah respectively, the interior region remained largely independent from either kingdoms.[^cite_note-63] The [Sultanate of Bulungan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Bulungan)'s influence was limited to the Tawau area,[^cite_note-64] which came under the influence of the Sulu Sultanate before gaining its own rule after the 1878 treaty between the British and Spanish governments.[^cite_note-65]

### British North Borneo

Main articles: [North Borneo Chartered Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Borneo_Chartered_Company), [North Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Borneo), and [Madrid Protocol of 1885](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Protocol_of_1885)

Concession of Sabah



The first concession treaty was signed by Sultan [Abdul Momin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Momin) of Brunei on 29 December 1877.

[^cite_note-century-24]



The second concession treaty was signed by Sultan [Jamal ul-Azam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamal_ul-Azam) of Sulu on 22 January 1878.

[^cite_note-ssc-60]

In 1761, [Alexander Dalrymple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Dalrymple), an officer of the British [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company), concluded an agreement with the Sultan of Sulu to allow him to set up a trading post in northern Borneo, although this was to prove a failure.[^cite_note-66] Following the [British occupation of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_occupation_of_Manila) in 1763, the British freed Sultan [Alimuddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azim_ud-Din_I_of_Sulu) of Sulu from the Spanish and allowed him to return to his throne;[^cite_note-Sulu_Archipelago-67] this was welcomed by the Sulu people and by 1765, Dalrymple managed to obtain [Balambangan Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balambangan_Island) off the north coast of Borneo, having concluded a Treaty of Alliance and Commerce with the Sultan Alimuddin as a sign of gratitude for the British aid.[^cite_note-Brunei_history-59][^cite_note-Sulu_Archipelago-67] A small British factory was then established in 1773 on the island.[^cite_note-Brunei_history-59] The British saw the island as a suitable location to control the trade route in the East, capable of diverting trade from the Spanish port of [Manila](184334.md) and the Dutch port of [Batavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batavia,_Dutch_East_Indies) especially with its strategic location between the [South China Sea](74209.md) and [Sulu Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulu_Sea).[^cite_note-Brunei_history-59] But the British abandoned the island two years later when [Sulu pirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piracy_in_the_Sulu_Sea) began attacking.[^cite_note-sabah_edu-46] This forced the British to seek refuge in Brunei in 1774, and to temporarily abandon their attempts to find alternative sites for the factory.[^cite_note-Brunei_history-59] Although an attempt was made in 1803 to turn Balambangan into a military station,[^cite_note-sabah_edu-46] the British did not re-establish any further trading posts in the region until [Stamford Raffles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stamford_Raffles) [founded Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_years_of_modern_Singapore) in 1819.[^cite_note-Brunei_history-59]

In 1846, the Sultan of Brunei ceded the island of [Labuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labuan) on the west coast of Sabah to Britain through the [Treaty of Labuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Labuan), and in 1848 it became a [British Crown Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Colony_of_Labuan).[^cite_note-sabah_edu-46] Seeing the presence of British in Labuan, the American consul in Brunei, Claude Lee Moses, obtained a ten-year lease in 1865 for a piece of land in northern Borneo. Moses then passed the land to the [American Trading Company of Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Trading_Company_of_Borneo), owned by [Joseph William Torrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_William_Torrey), [Thomas Bradley Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Bradley_Harris) and Chinese investors.[^cite_note-sabah_edu-46][^cite_note-Fitzgerald2016-68] The company choose [Kimanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimanis) (which they renamed "Ellena") as a site for a settlement. Requests for financial backing from the US government proved futile and the settlement was later abandoned. Before he left, Torrey managed to sell all his rights to the Austrian Consul in Hong Kong, [Gustav von Overbeck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_Overbeck). Overbeck then went to Brunei, where he met the [Temenggong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temenggong) to renew the [concession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concession_\(territory\)).[^cite_note-Fitzgerald2016-68] Brunei agreed to cede all territory in northern Borneo under its control, with the Sultan receiving an annual payment of 12,000 [Spanish dollars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_dollar), while the Temenggong received a sum of 3,000.[^cite_note-Brunei_history-59]

In 1872, the Sultanate of Sulu granted use of an area of land in the Sandakan Bay to William Frederick Schuck, a former agent of the German consular service who had lived on the Sulu island of [Jolo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jolo) since 1864. The arrival of German warship [SMS *Nymphe*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SMS_Nymphe_\(1863\)) at the Sulu Sea in 1872 to investigate the [Sulu-Spanish conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93Moro_conflict) made the sultanate believe Schuck was connected with the [German government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Germany).[^cite_note-69] The sultanate authorised Schuck to establish a trading port to monopolise the [rattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rattan) trade in the northeast coast, where Schuck could operate freely, without the Spanish blockade.[^cite_note-70] He continued this operation until this land also was ceded to Overbeck, with the Sultan receiving an annual payment of \$5,000, by a treaty signed in 1878.[^cite_note-Brunei_history-59]

After a series of transfers, Overbeck tried to sell the territory to [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire), [Austria-Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria-Hungary) and [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy) but they rejected his offer.[^cite_note-Fitzgerald2016-68] Overbeck then co-operated with the British Dent brothers ([Alfred Dent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Dent) and Edward Dent) for financial backing to develop the land, with the Dent company persuading him that any investors would need guarantees of British military and diplomatic support.[^cite_note-Fitzgerald2016-68] Overbeck agreed to this co-operation, especially with regard to the counterclaims of the Sultan of Sulu, part of whose territory in the Sulu Archipelago had been occupied by Spain.[^cite_note-Fitzgerald2016-68] Overbeck, however, withdrew in 1879 and his treaty rights were transferred to Alfred Dent, who in 1881 formed the North Borneo Provisional Association Ltd to administer the territory.[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73] In the following year, [Kudat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kudat) was made its capital but due to frequent pirate attacks, the capital was moved to [Sandakan](1732138.md) in 1884.[^cite_note-Hutton2000-33] To prevent further disputes over intervention, the governments of the United Kingdom, Spain and Germany signed the [Madrid Protocol of 1885](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Protocol_of_1885), recognising the sovereignty of the [King of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Spain) over the Sulu Archipelago in return for the relinquishment of all Spanish claims over northern Borneo.[^cite_note-74] The arrival of the company brought prosperity to the residents of northern Borneo, with the company allowing indigenous communities to continue their traditional lifestyles, but imposing laws against [headhunting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Headhunting), [ethnic feuds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_conflict), [slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery), and piracy.[^cite_note-Skutsch2013-75][^cite_note-bnbm-76] North Borneo then became a [protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectorate) of the United Kingdom in 1888 despite facing local resistance from 1894 to 1900 by [Mat Salleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mat_Salleh_Rebellion) and [Antanum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antanum) in 1915.[^cite_note-sabah_edu-46][^cite_note-bnbm-76] The Sultanate of Sulu [continued to exist after 1915](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu_after_1915) as a ceremonial institution.[^cite_note-\:0-77]

### Second World War

Main articles: [Battle of Borneo (1941–1942)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Borneo_\(1941%E2%80%931942\)), [Japanese occupation of British Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_British_Borneo), and [Battle of North Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_North_Borneo)



[Japanese civilians and soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_prisoners_of_war_in_World_War_II) prior to their embarkation to Jesselton after their surrender to the [Australian Imperial Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Australian_Imperial_Force) (AIF) in [Tawau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawau) on 21 October 1945

The Japanese forces landed in Labuan on 3 January 1942,[^cite_note-78] during the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War), and later invaded the rest of northern Borneo.[^cite_note-sabah_edu-46] From 1942 to 1945, Japanese forces occupied North Borneo, along with most of the rest of the island, as part of the [Empire of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan). The British saw Japanese advances in the area as motivated by political and territorial ambitions rather than economic factors.[^cite_note-79] The residing British and the locals were compelled to obey and gave in to the brutality of the Japanese.[^cite_note-Three_Came_Home-80] The occupation drove many people from coastal towns to the interior, fleeing the Japanese and seeking food.[^cite_note-Lim2008-81] The [Malays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malays_\(ethnic_group\)) generally appeared to be favoured by the Japanese, although some of them faced repression, while other groups such as the Chinese and indigenous peoples were severely repressed.[^cite_note-82] The Chinese were already resisting the Japanese occupation, especially with the [Sino-Japanese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Sino-Japanese_War) in mainland China.[^cite_note-83] Local Chinese formed a resistance, known as the Kinabalu Guerillas, led by [Albert Kwok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Kwok), with broad support from various ethnic groups in northern Borneo such as [Dusun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dusun_people), Murut, Suluk and Illanun peoples. The movement was also supported by [Mustapha Harun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustapha_Harun).[^cite_note-Gin2010-84] Kwok along with many other sympathisers were, however, executed after the Japanese foiled their movement in the [Jesselton Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesselton_Revolt).[^cite_note-Lim2008-81][^cite_note-85]

As part of the [Borneo campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borneo_campaign) to retake the territory, [Allied forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) bombed most of the major towns under Japanese control, including Sandakan, which was razed to the ground. The Japanese ran a brutal [prisoner of war](25008.md) camp known as [Sandakan camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandakan_camp).[^cite_note-86] The majority of the POWs were British and Australian soldiers captured after the [fall of Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayan_campaign) and [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Singapore).[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88] The prisoners suffered inhuman conditions, and amidst continuous Allied bombardments, the Japanese forced them to march to [Ranau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranau_District), about 260 kilometres (160 mi) away, in an event known as the [Sandakan Death March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandakan_Death_March).[^cite_note-89] The number of prisoners were reduced to 2,345, with many of them killed en route either by [friendly fire](202484.md) or by the Japanese. Only six of the several hundred Australian prisoners lived to see the war's end.[^cite_note-90] In addition, of the total of 17,488 [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_people) labourers brought in by the Japanese during the occupation, only 1,500 survived mainly due to starvation, harsh working conditions and maltreatment.[^cite_note-Lim2008-81] In March 1945, Australian forces launched [Operation Agas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Agas) to gather intelligence in the region and launch guerrilla warfare against the Japanese.[^cite_note-AWM-91] The [Australian Imperial Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Australian_Imperial_Force) initiated the [Battle of North Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_North_Borneo) on 10 June 1945.[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93] Japan's remaining forces surrendered on 2 September 1945, following the [atomic bombings of Hiroshima and Nagasaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bombings_of_Hiroshima_and_Nagasaki).[^cite_note-94]

### British crown colony

Main article: [Crown Colony of North Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Colony_of_North_Borneo)

After the Japanese surrender, North Borneo was administered by the [British Military Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Military_Administration_\(Borneo\)) and on 15 July 1946 became a [British Crown colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Colony_of_North_Borneo).[^cite_note-sabah_edu-46][^cite_note-cc-95] The [Crown Colony of Labuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Colony_of_Labuan) was integrated into this new colony. During the ceremony, both the [Union Jack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Jack) and [Flag of the Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_the_Republic_of_China) were raised from the bullet-ridden Jesselton Survey Hall building.[^cite_note-cc-95] The Chinese were represented by Philip Lee who had been part of the resistance movement against the Japanese and who eventually supported the transfer of power to the Crown colony.[^cite_note-cc-95] He said: "Let their blood be the pledge of what we wish to be—[His Majesty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_VI)'s most devoted subjects."[^cite_note-cc-95]

Due to massive destruction in Sandakan during the war, Jesselton was chosen to replace the capital while the Crown continued to rule North Borneo until 1963. The Crown colony government established many departments to oversee the welfare of its residents and to revive the economy of North Borneo after the war.[^cite_note-96] Upon [Philippine independence in 1946](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1946%E2%80%9365\)), seven of the British-controlled [Turtle Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turtle_Islands,_Tawi-Tawi) (including [Cagayan de Tawi-Tawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mapun,_Tawi-Tawi) and [Mangsee Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangsee_Islands)) off the north coast of Borneo were ceded to the Philippines as had been negotiated by the American and British colonial governments.[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98]

### Malaysia

Main articles: [Malaysia Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia_Agreement), [20-point agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20-point_agreement), and [Indonesia–Malaysia confrontation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesia%E2%80%93Malaysia_confrontation)



[Donald Stephens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuad_Stephens) (left) declaring the forming of the Federation of Malaysia at [Merdeka Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merdeka_Square,_Kota_Kinabalu), Jesselton on 16 September 1963. Together with him was the then [Deputy Prime Minister of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_Malaysia) [Tun Abdul Razak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Razak_Hussein) (right) and [Mustapha Harun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustapha_Harun) (second right).

On 31 August 1963, North Borneo [attained self-governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Day).[^cite_note-tna1-5][^cite_note-tna2-6][^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100] The [Cobbold Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cobbold_Commission) had been set up in 1962, to determine whether the people of Sabah and Sarawak favoured the proposed union of a new federation called Malaysia, and found that the union was generally favoured by the people.[^cite_note-101] Most ethnic community leaders of Sabah, namely, Mustapha Harun representing the native Muslims, [Donald Stephens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuad_Stephens) representing the non-Muslim natives, and Khoo Siak Chew representing the Chinese, would eventually support the union.[^cite_note-Gin2010-84][^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103] After a discussion culminating in the [Malaysia Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia_Agreement) and [20-point agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20-point_agreement), on 16 September 1963 North Borneo (as Sabah) was united with Malaya, Sarawak and Singapore, to form the independent [Malaysia](3607937.md).[^cite_note-104][^cite_note-105]

From before the formation of Malaysia until 1966, [Indonesia](14579.md) adopted a hostile policy towards British-backed Malaya, leading to the [Indonesia–Malaysia confrontation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konfrontasi) after Malaysia was established.[^cite_note-106] The war stemmed from what Indonesian president [Sukarno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukarno) perceived as an expansion of British influence in the region and his intention to wrest control over all of Borneo under the [Greater Indonesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Indonesia) concept.[^cite_note-107] Meanwhile, the [Philippines](23440.md), beginning with president [Diosdado Macapagal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diosdado_Macapagal) on 22 June 1962, [claimed Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Borneo_dispute) through the heirs of the Sultanate of Sulu.[^cite_note-PH_claim-108][^cite_note-PH_claim_research-109] Macapagal, considering Sabah to be property of the Sultanate of Sulu, saw the attempt to integrate Sabah, Sarawak and Brunei into the Federation of Malaysia as "trying to impose authority of Malaya into these states".[^cite_note-PH_claim-108]

Following the successful formation of Malaysia, Donald Stephens became the first chief minister of Sabah. The first [Yang di-Pertua Negara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yang_di-Pertuan_Negara#Sabah) (later restyled [Yang di-Pertua Negeri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yang_di-Pertua_Negeri_of_Sabah) in 1976) was Mustapha Harun.[^cite_note-110] The leaders of Sabah demanded that their [freedom of religion](46006.md) be respected, that all lands in the territory be under the power of state government, and that native customs and traditions be respected and upheld by the federal government; declaring that in return Sabahans would pledge their loyalty to the Malaysian federal government. An [oath stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keningau_Oath_Stone) was officiated by Donald Stephens on 31 August 1964 in [Keningau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keningau) as a remembrance to the agreement and promise for reference in the future.[^cite_note-oath_stone_story-111] Sabah held its first state election in 1967.[^cite_note-112] In the same year, the name of the state capital was changed from "Jesselton" to "[Kota Kinabalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kota_Kinabalu)".[^cite_note-113]

An [airplane crash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Sabah_Air_GAF_Nomad_crash) on 6 June 1976 killed Stephens along with four other state cabinet ministers.[^cite_note-114] On 14 June 1976, the state [government of Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Sabah) led by the new chief minister [Harris Salleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harris_Salleh) signed an agreement with [Petronas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petronas), the federal government-owned oil and gas company, granting it the right to extract and earn revenue from petroleum found in the territorial waters of Sabah in exchange for 5% in annual revenue as [royalties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royalties) based on the 1974 Petroleum Development Act.[^cite_note-KincaidShah2007-115] The state government of Sabah ceded [Labuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labuan) to the Malaysian federal government, and Labuan became a [federal territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Territory_\(Malaysia\)) on 16 April 1984.[^cite_note-116] In 2000, the state capital Kota Kinabalu was granted [city status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_status), making it the [6th city in Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Malaysia) and the first city in the state.[^cite_note-117] Prior to a [territorial dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ligitan_and_Sipadan_dispute) between Indonesia and Malaysia since 1969 over two islands of [Ligitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ligitan) and [Sipadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sipadan) in the [Celebes Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celebes_Sea), the [International Court of Justice](14918.md) (ICJ) made a final decision to award both islands to Malaysia in 2002 based on their "effective occupation".[^cite_note-Justice2003-118][^cite_note-court_result-119]

In February 2013, Sabah's [Lahad Datu District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahad_Datu_District) was [penetrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Lahad_Datu_standoff) by followers of [Jamalul Kiram III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamalul_Kiram_III), the self-proclaimed [Sultan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sultans_of_Sulu) of [Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu). In response, Malaysian military forces were deployed to the region, which resulted in 72 deaths (56 Sultanate militants, nine Malaysian security personnel, and six civilians). Following the elimination of insurgents, an [Eastern Sabah Security Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Sabah_Security_Command) was established.[^cite_note-120][^cite_note-LD_2013-121][^cite_note-PLD_2013-122]

## Politics

### Government

See also: [Government of Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Sabah), [Cabinet of Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Sabah), [Sabah State Legislative Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_State_Legislative_Assembly), [Politics of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Malaysia), and [Table of precedence of Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_of_precedence_of_Sabah)



The [State Administrative Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_State_Administrative_Centre) (right), behind the [Wisma Innoprise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisma_Innoprise) (left)

Sabah (together with its neighbour Sarawak) has a greater level of autonomy in administration, immigration, and judiciary which differentiates it from the Malaysian Peninsula states. The [Yang di-Pertua Negeri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yang_di-Pertua_Negeri) is the [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state) although its functions are largely ceremonial.[^cite_note-state_government_structure-123] Next in the hierarchy are the [state legislative assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dewan_Undangan_Negeri) and the state cabinet.[^cite_note-Group-19][^cite_note-state_government_structure-123] The [chief minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chief_Ministers_of_Sabah) is the [head of government](37570.md) as well the leader of the state cabinet.[^cite_note-state_government_structure-123] The legislature is based on the [Westminster system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_system) and therefore the chief minister is appointed based on his or her ability to command the majority of the state assembly.[^cite_note-Group-19][^cite_note-124] While local authorities being fully appointed by the state government owing to the suspension of local elections by the federal government. Legislation regarding state elections is within the powers of the federal government and not the state.[^cite_note-Group-19] The assembly meets at the state capital, Kota Kinabalu. Members of the state assembly are elected from 73 constituencies which are delineated by the [Election Commission of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Commission_of_Malaysia) and do not necessarily have the same voter population sizes.[^cite_note-125] A general election for representatives in the state assembly must be held every five years, when the seats are subject of [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage) for all citizens above 21 years of age. Sabah is also represented in the [federal parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Malaysia) by 25 members elected from the same number of constituencies.

Prior to the formation of Malaysia in 1963, the then [North Borneo interim government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Colony_of_North_Borneo) submitted a [20-point agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20-point_agreement) to the [Malayan government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Malaya) as conditions before North Borneo would join the federation. Subsequently, North Borneo legislative assembly agreed on the formation of Malaysia on the conditions that North Borneo's rights would be safeguarded. North Borneo then entered Malaysia as an [autonomous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_entity) state with autonomous laws in immigration control and [Native Customary Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_title) (NCR), and the territory name was changed to "Sabah". However, under the administration of the [United Sabah National Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Sabah_National_Organisation) (USNO) led by Mustapha Harun, this autonomy has been gradually eroded with federal government influence and hegemony with a popular belief among Sabahans that both USNO and UMNO have been working together in permitting [illegal immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_immigrants_in_Malaysia) from the southern Philippines and Indonesia to stay in the state and become citizens to vote for Muslim parties.[^cite_note-Sadiq2008-126] This was continued under the [Sabah People's United Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_People's_United_Front) (BERJAYA) administration led by Harris Salleh with a total of 73,000 [Filipino refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_Philippines) from the southern Philippines were registered.[^cite_note-127] In addition, the cession of Labuan island to federal government by the Sabah state government under BERJAYA rule and unequal sharing and exploitation of Sabah's resources of petroleum have become grievances often raised by Sabahans, which has resulted in strong anti-federal sentiments and even occasional call for [secession](144732.md) from the federation among the people of Sabah.[^cite_note-Lim2008-81] Those who spread secession agenda often landed in law enforcement hand due to the controversial ISA act, such as [1991 Sabah political arrests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Sabah_political_arrests).[^cite_note-128]



The [Sabah State Legislative Assembly Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_State_Legislative_Assembly_Building) in Kota Kinabalu

Until the [2008 Malaysian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_general_election,_2008), Sabah along with the states of [Kelantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelantan) and [Terengganu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terengganu), were the only three states in Malaysia that had ever been ruled by [opposition parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_\(Malaysia\)) not part of the ruling BN coalition. Under [Joseph Pairin Kitingan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Pairin_Kitingan), PBS formed the state government after winning the [1985 state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_state_election,_1985) and ruled Sabah until 1994. In the [1994 state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_state_election,_1994), despite PBS winning the elections, subsequent cross-overs of PBS assembly members to the BN component party resulted in BN having the majority of seats and hence took over the helm of the state government.[^cite_note-129] A unique feature of Sabah politics was a policy initiated by Prime Minister [Mahathir Mohamad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahathir_Mohamad) in 1994 whereby the chief minister's post is rotated among the coalition parties every two years regardless of the party in power at the time, thus theoretically giving an equal amount of time for each major ethnic group to rule the state. However, in practice, this system was problematic as it is too short for any leader to carry out long-term plans.[^cite_note-130] This practice was then since stopped.[^cite_note-131] Political intervention by the federal authorities, for example, an introduction and later abolition of the chief minister's post and earlier [PBS-BERJAYA conflict in 1985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Sabah_riots), along with co-opting rival factions in East Malaysia, are examples of political tactics used by the then UMNO-led federal government to control and manage the autonomous power of the Borneo states.[^cite_note-132] The federal government however tend to view that these actions are justifiable as the display of [parochialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parish_pump_politics) among East Malaysians is not in harmony with nation building. This complicated Federal-State relationship has become a source of major contention in Sabah politics.[^cite_note-Lim2008-81]

[Juhar Mahiruddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juhar_Mahiruddin)

[Yang di-Pertua Negeri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yang_di-Pertua_Negeri_of_Sabah), [Musa Aman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musa_Aman)

[Shafie Apdal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hajiji_Noor)

[Chief Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chief_Ministers_of_Sabah), [Hajiji Noor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hajiji_Noor)

In the [2018 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_general_election,_2018), [Shafie Apdal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shafie_Apdal)'s [Sabah Heritage Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Heritage_Party) (WARISAN) secured an electoral pact with the [Democratic Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Action_Party_\(Malaysia\)) (DAP) and [People's Justice Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Justice_Party_\(Malaysia\)) (PKR) of the [Pakatan Harapan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakatan_Harapan) (PH) coalition. On 9 May 2018, this coalition and the Barisan Nasional ended in a tie.[^cite_note-133] However, as six BN elected representatives crossed over to WARISAN,[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135] and after a short-lived constitutional crisis,[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] a coalition of WARISAN, DAP and PKR formed a majority government on 12 May 2018 and became effective since that day.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141] In conjunction with the celebration of [Malaysia Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia_Day) in 2018 under the new government, Prime Minister Mahathir has promised to restore Sabah (together with Sarawak) status as an equal partner to Malaya who together forming the Malaysian federation in accordance to the Malaysia Agreement.[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143] However, through the process of the proposed [amendment to the Constitution of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposed_2019_amendment_to_the_Constitution_of_Malaysia) in 2019, the first bill for the amendment failed to pass following the failure to reach two-thirds majority support (148 votes) in the Parliament with only 138 agreed with the move while 59 abstained from the voting.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145] Subsequently, a [second bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_amendment_to_the_Constitution_of_Malaysia) for the amendment was tabled in 2021 and was passed unanimously by the Malaysian Parliament.[^cite_note-146]

### Administrative division

Main articles: [Divisions of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divisions_of_Malaysia), [Districts of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Malaysia), [List of local governments in Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_local_governments_in_Malaysia), and [Local government in Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_Sabah)

Sabah consists of five administrative divisions, which are in turn divided into 30 districts. For each district, the state government appoints a village headman (known as *ketua kampung*) for each village. The administrative divisions were inherited from the provinces of the British administration.[^cite_note-147] During the British rule, a [Resident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resident_\(title\)) was appointed to govern each division and provided with a palace (*Istana*).[^cite_note-148] The post of the Resident was abolished and replaced with district officers for each of the district when North Borneo became part of Malaysia. As in the rest of Malaysia, local government comes under the purview of state government.[^cite_note-Group-19] However, ever since the suspension of local government elections in the midst of the [Malayan Emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayan_Emergency), which was much less intense in Sabah than it was in the rest of the country, there have been no local elections. Local authorities have their officials appointed by the executive council of the state government.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150]

## Security

Main articles: [Cross border attacks in Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross_border_attacks_in_Sabah), [Eastern Sabah Security Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Sabah_Security_Command), and [Eastern Sabah Security Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Sabah_Security_Zone)



A [Malaysian Army](1966311.md) soldier armed with [Colt M4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_carbine) standing guard in Sabah east coast as part of the [Eastern Sabah Security Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Sabah_Security_Command) (ESSCOM)

The Ninth Schedule of the Constitution of Malaysia states that the [Malaysian federal government](9660028.md) is solely responsible for foreign policy and military forces in the country.[^cite_note-152] Before the formation of Malaysia, North Borneo security was the responsibility of Great Britain, Australia and [New Zealand](4913064.md).[^cite_note-153] In the wake of threats of "annexation" from the Philippines after President [Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos) signed a bill by including Sabah as part the Republic of the Philippines on its maritime baselines in the [Act of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_the_Philippines) on 18 September 1968,[^cite_note-Wah1974-154] the British responds in the next day by sending their [Hawker Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawker_Hunter) [fighter-bomber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fighter-bomber) jets to Kota Kinabalu with the jets stopped over at the [Clark Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clark_Air_Base) not far from the Philippines capital of Manila.[^cite_note-Chin1983-155] [British Army](4887.md) senior officer [Michael Carver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Carver,_Baron_Carver) then reminded the Philippines that Britain would honour its obligations under the [Anglo-Malayan Defence Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Malayan_Defence_Agreement) (AMDA) if fighting broke out.[^cite_note-Chin1983-155] In addition, a large flotilla of British warships would sail to Philippines waters near Sabah en route from Singapore along with the participation of [ANZUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ANZUS) forces.[^cite_note-Chin1983-155] The AMDA treaty have since been replaced by the [Five Power Defence Arrangements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Power_Defence_Arrangements) (FPDA) although the present treaty does not include East Malaysian states as its main priority, British security protection intervention can still be included over the two states.[^cite_note-Wah1974-154][^cite_note-fpda-156] Citing in 1971 when British Prime Minister [Edward Heath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Heath) been asked in [Parliament of London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Westminster) on what threats the British intended to counter under the FPDA, the Prime Minister replied: to "forces outside \[Malaysia] in southern [Thailand](30128.md) and north of the Malaysian border".[^cite_note-157]

The area in eastern Sabah facing the southern Philippines and northern Indonesia have since been put under the [Eastern Sabah Security Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Sabah_Security_Command) (ESSCOM) and [Eastern Sabah Security Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Sabah_Security_Zone) (ESSZONE) following the infiltration of militants, [illegal immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_immigrants) and [smuggling](28645.md) of goods and subsidies items into and from the southern Philippines and Indonesia.[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159]

### Territorial disputes

Main articles: [Ligitan and Sipadan dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ligitan_and_Sipadan_dispute), [Spratly Islands dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spratly_Islands_dispute), [North Borneo dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Borneo_dispute), and [Malaysia Sulu case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia_Sulu_case)



Map of the British North Borneo with the yellow area covered the Philippine claim to eastern Sabah, presented by the Philippine Government to ICJ on 25 June 2001

[^cite_note-160]

Sabah has had several territorial disputes with neighbouring Indonesia and the Philippines. In 2002, both Malaysia and Indonesia submitted to arbitration by the ICJ on a territorial dispute over the Ligitan and Sipadan islands which were later won by Malaysia.[^cite_note-Justice2003-118][^cite_note-court_result-119] There are also several other disputes yet to be settled with Indonesia over the overlapping claims on the [Ambalat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambalat) continental shelf in the Celebes Sea and land border dispute between Sabah and [North Kalimantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Kalimantan).[^cite_note-161] Malaysia's claim over a portion of the [Spratly Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spratly_Islands) is also based on sharing a continental shelf with Sabah.[^cite_note-162]

The Philippines claims much of eastern Sabah.[^cite_note-Chinese_records-42][^cite_note-myph-58][^cite_note-163] It claims that the territory is connected with the Sultanate of Sulu and was only leased to the North Borneo Chartered Company in 1878 with the Sultanate's sovereignty never being relinquished.[^cite_note-PH_claim_research-109] Malaysia however, considers this dispute as a "non-issue", as it interprets the 1878 agreement as that of [cession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cession) and that it deems that the residents of Sabah had exercised their right to [self-determination](29269.md) when they joined to form the Malaysian federation in 1963.[^cite_note-164] A group of 200 armed Filipinos identifying themselves as the Royal Security Forces of the Sultanate of Sulu and North Borneo landed in the district of [Lahad Datu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahad_Datu_District) and took control of the Tanduo village in 2013, with the objective of reinforcing the Philippine claim over the eastern region of Sabah. This Lahad Datu incident resulted in the deaths of 56 members of the Sulu group, nine Malaysian security force members including six civilians.[^cite_note-LD_2013-121][^cite_note-PLD_2013-122]

Before this incident, Malaysia continued to pay an annual cession payment amounting to roughly \$1,000 to the indirect heirs of the Sultan honouring an 1878 agreement, where [North Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Borneo) – today's Sabah – was conceded by the Sultan of Sulu to a British company.[^cite_note-sultanate_forum_shopping-165] However, the Malaysian government halted the payments after this incident. As a result, the self-proclaimed Sulu heirs pursued this case for legal arbitration vis-a-vis the original commercial deal. Since then, the Sulu claimants have been accused of "[forum shopping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forum_shopping)".[^cite_note-sultanate_forum_shopping-165] In 2017, the heirs showed their intention to start arbitration in Spain and asked for \$32.2 billion in compensation. In 2019, Malaysia responded for the first time. The attorney general at the time offered to start making yearly payments again and to pay 48,000 Malaysian ringgit (about \$10,400) for past dues and interest, but only if the heirs gave up their claim.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167] The heirs did not accept this offer and the case, led by Spanish arbiter [Gonzalo Stampa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonzalo_Stampa), continued without Malaysia being involved. In February 2022, Gonzalo Stampa awarded US\$14.9 billion to the Sultan of Sulu's heirs, who then attempted to enforce the award against Malaysian state-owned assets around the world.[^cite_note-168] It is noteworthy that on 27 June 2023, the Hague Court of Appeal dismissed the Sulus' bid and ruled in favour of the Malaysian government, which hailed the decision as a "landmark victory".[^cite_note-169] In 2024 Stampa was convicted of contempt of court for "knowingly disobeying rulings and orders from the Madrid High Court of Justice", and sentenced to six months in prison.[^cite_note-170] On 3 October 2024, Malaysia's Federal Court upheld the death sentences of seven Filipino men involved in the 2013 Lahad Datu invasion, which had resulted in the deaths of nine Malaysian security personnel.[^cite_note-171] The ruling was seen as a step toward ensuring justice and strengthening national security in Malaysia.[^cite_note-172]

On 7 November 2024, the French Court of Cassation—the highest court in the French judicial system—annulled a \$15 billion arbitration ruling against Malaysia.[^cite_note-trib-173] This decision marked a significant legal victory for Malaysia and reinforced its sovereignty in a dispute with the self-proclaimed Sulu heirs.[^cite_note-trib-173] The ruling highlighted irregularities in the arbitration process led by [Gonzalo Stampa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonzalo_Stampa) and raised concerns about practices such as forum shopping and unregulated litigation funding in European courts.[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-\$15B_arbitration_case-175] The French court's decision was deemed a significant "win" for Malaysia that effectively marked the end of the Sulu case by several publications, including Law\.com and [Law360](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law360).[^cite_note-\$15B_arbitration_case-175][^cite_note-176] [Keith Ellison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keith_Ellison), former vice-chairman of the Democratic National Committee and [Minnesota attorney general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_of_Minnesota), pointed out that the case highlighted the enormous scope for "corruption", irresponsible profiteering, and foreign influence operations to subvert arbitration proceedings".[^cite_note-177]

The French court had declared the arbitration clause in the case invalid. It mainatined that the agreement appointed the British Consul General in Brunei as arbitrator, and since the position no longer existed, the arbitration could not take place.[^cite_note-\:2-178] The verdict underscored the need for international arbitration agreements to be interpreted in accordance with the principles of good faith and utility, "without reference to the law of any State".[^cite_note-179]

Professor Caroline Kleiner of the University of Paris Cité called the ruling a major shift in arbitration law and hearing. In her review published in Kluwer Law International, she said the decision would help French courts participate in the development of international law on the enforcement of foreign arbitral awards per a universalist approach, rather than the trend of exceptionalism that is currently being pursued.[^cite_note-180]

Following Malaysia's legal victory in the French Court, [Paul Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Cohen_\(lawyer\)) argued that the ruling allows the Sulu heirs to lease Sabah to other nations, such as China and the Philippines. He also filed a fresh \$18 billion claim against Spain, alleging that his clients had been denied justice. Cohen also suggested that accepting the French court's decision implies recognition of the Sulu Sultanate descendants' sovereignty over Sabah, which Malaysia disputes.[^cite_note-181] In response, Malaysia's [Minister of Law and Institutional Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_in_the_Prime_Minister's_Department) in the Prime Minister Department, [Azalina Othman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azalina_Othman_Said) dismissed Cohen's statements as baseless and reaffirmed Sabah's status as part of Malaysia, citing historical and legal foundations such as the Cobbold Commission and the 1963 referendum.[^cite_note-182] The Philippine claim can be originated based on three historical events; such as the Brunei Civil War from 1660 until 1673, treaty between [Dutch East Indies](23476997.md) and the [Bulungan Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulungan_Sultanate) in 1850 and treaty between Sultan [Jamal ul-Azam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamal_ul-Azam) with Overbeck in 1878.[^cite_note-myph-58][^cite_note-SullivanLeong1981-183]

Further attempts by several Filipino politicians such as Ferdinand Marcos to "destabilise" Sabah proved to be futile and led to the [Jabidah massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabidah_massacre) in [Corregidor Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corregidor_Island), Philippines.[^cite_note-Chin1983-155][^cite_note-184] As a consequence, this led the Malaysian government to once supporting the [insurgency in southern Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moro_conflict_in_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-hts-185][^cite_note-186] Although the Philippine claim to Sabah has not been actively pursued for some years, some Filipino politicians have promised to bring it up again,[^cite_note-187] while the Malaysian government have asked the Philippines not to [threaten ties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia%E2%80%93Philippines_relations) over such an issue.[^cite_note-188] To further discourage pursuit of the claim the Malaysian government passed a [barter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barter) trade ban, at the behest of the [Royal Malaysia Police](11760718.md) and the [Malaysian Deputy Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_Malaysia), between Malaysia and the Philippines as it was seen to only benefit one side while threatening the security of the state.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190] The ban was positively received by many Sabahans, although there was opposition from [other political parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_\(Malaysia\)) as well as from the residents of neighbouring Philippine islands due to a sharp rise in living costs after the ban took effect.[^cite_note-191] Barter trade activity was resumed on 1 February 2017 upon the agreement by both Malaysian and the Philippine authorities to fortify their respective borders with increased surveillance and security enforcement.[^cite_note-economy_resumed-192][^cite_note-193] Despite the return of barter trade activity, the state of Sabah maintained that they will remain vigilant in trading with the Philippines.[^cite_note-194] In 2016, Philippine President [Rodrigo Duterte](7407293.md) and Malaysian Prime Minister [Najib Razak](523641.md) have agreed to set aside the two countries' dispute over Sabah for the meantime.[^cite_note-195]

## Geography



Sabah is located south of the [typhoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon) belt, making it insusceptible to the devastating effects of the typhoons which frequently batter the neighbouring [Philippines](23440.md),

[^cite_note-land_below_the_wind-25]

&#x20;such as the [Typhoon Haiyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon_Haiyan) in 2013.

[^cite_note-196]

The total land area of Sabah is nearly 73,904 square kilometres (28,534 sq mi)[^cite_note-statistics-197] surrounded by the South China Sea in the west, Sulu Sea in the northeast and Celebes Sea in the southeast.[^cite_note-sbh-2] Sabah has a total of 1,743 kilometres (1,083 mi) coastline, of which 295.5 kilometres (183.6 mi) have been [eroding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastal_erosion).[^cite_note-198] Because of Sabah coastline facing three seas, the state receive an extensive marine resources.[^cite_note-fishdept-199] In 1961, Sabah including neighbouring [Sarawak](28258.md), which had been included in the [International Maritime Organization](14986.md) (IMO) through the participation of the United Kingdom, became joint associate members of the IMO.[^cite_note-200] Its [exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone) (EEZ) is much larger towards the South China Sea and Celebes Sea than to the Sulu Sea.[^cite_note-geo-201] The state coastline is covered with [mangrove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrove) and [nipah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nypa_fruticans) forests. The mangroves cover about 331,325 hectares of the state land and constitute 57% of the total mangroves in the country.[^cite_note-geo-201] Both coastal areas in the west coast and east coast are entirely dominating by sand beaches, while in sheltered areas the sand was mixed with mud.[^cite_note-202] The northern area of the [Tip of Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tip_of_Borneo) has a type of [pocket beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pocket_beach).[^cite_note-203] The areas in the west coast has a large freshwater [wetlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wetland), with the Klias Peninsula hosts a large area of tidal wetlands[^cite_note-Mojiol2006-204] and a wetland centre known as the [Kota Kinabalu Wetland Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kota_Kinabalu_Wetland_Centre) was designated as a [Ramsar site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramsar_site) in 2016.[^cite_note-205] The western part of Sabah is generally mountainous, containing three highest peak. The main [mountain ranges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_range) is the [Crocker Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocker_Range) with several mountains varying height from about 1,000 metres to 4,000 metres. Adjacent to the Crocker Range is the [Trus Madi Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trus_Madi_Range) with [Mount Trus Madi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Trus_Madi), with a height of 2,642 metres.[^cite_note-206] The highest peak is the [Mount Kinabalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Kinabalu), with a height around 4,095 metres.[^cite_note-207] It is [one of the highest peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Southeast_Asian_mountains) between the [Himalayas](37966.md) and [New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Guinea).[^cite_note-208] Located not far from Mount Kinabalu is [Mount Tambuyukon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Tambuyukon), with a height of 2,579 metres.[^cite_note-209]

These mountains and hills are traversed by an extensive network of river valleys and are in most cases covered with dense rainforest. There are lower ranges of hills extending towards the western coasts, southern plains, and the interior or central part of Sabah. The central and eastern portions of Sabah are generally lower mountain ranges and plains with occasional hills. In the east coast located the Kinabatangan River, which is the second-longest river in Malaysia after [Rajang River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajang_River) in Sarawak with a length of 560 kilometres.[^cite_note-210] The river begins from the western ranges and snakes its way through the central region towards the east coast out into the Sulu Sea. Other major rivers including the Kalabakan River, [Kolopis River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolopis_River), Liwagu River, Padas River, Paitan River, Segama River and Sugut River, in addition to Babagon River, Bengkoka River, Kadamaian River, Kalumpang River, Kiulu River, Mawao River, Membakut River, Mesapol River, Nabawan River, Papar River, Pensiangan River, Tamparuli River and Wario River.[^cite_note-Tietze2007-211]

The land of Sabah is located in a [tropical geography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_geography) with [equatorial climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_rainforest_climate). It experiences two [monsoon](57630.md) seasons of northeast and southwest. The northeast monsoon occurs from November to March with heavy rains, while the southwest monsoon prevails from May to September with less rainfall.[^cite_note-Tietze2007-211] It also received two inter-monsoon season from April to May and September to October. The average daily temperature varies from 27 °C (81 °F) to 34 °C (93 °F), with a considerable amount of rain from 1,800 millimetres to 4,000 millimetres.[^cite_note-Tietze2007-211] The coastal areas occasionally experience severe storms as the state is situated south of the [typhoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon) belt.[^cite_note-Tietze2007-211] Due to its location is very close to the typhoon belt, Sabah experience the worst [Tropical Storm Greg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Pacific_typhoon_season) on 25 December 1996.[^cite_note-212] The storm left more than 100 people dead, with another 200–300 missing, 3,000–4,000 people left homeless.[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214] As Sabah also lies within the [Sunda Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunda_Plate) with a compression from the [Australian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Plate) and [Philippine Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Plate), it is prone to earthquake with the state itself have experienced three major earthquakes since 1923, with the [2015 earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Sabah_earthquake) being the latest major earthquake.[^cite_note-215] The Crocker Ranges together with Mount Kinabalu was formed since during the middle [Miocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miocene) period after being uplifted by the Sabah [Orogeny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orogeny) through compression.[^cite_note-216] There was some snow here in 1975 and 1993.[^cite_note-217]
