### Biodiversity

See also: [Biodiversity of Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_of_Borneo), [Deforestation in Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation_in_Borneo), and [List of nematodes in Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nematodes_in_Sabah)



[Blue-eared kingfisher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue-eared_kingfisher) in the lower [Kinabatangan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinabatangan_River) area. Kingfisher is also once a state bird of Sabah and featured in [one of its coat of arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_Sabah).

The [Semporna Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semporna_Peninsula) on the north-eastern coast of Sabah is identified as a hotspot of high marine biodiversity importance in the [Coral Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coral_Triangle).[^cite_note-Asaad18-218]

Map of Sabah showing tree-cover loss years, with forest remaining since 2000 in green and loss years shaded yellow through purple.

Tree-cover loss year in Sabah, 2001-2024, from the [Global Forest Change dataset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Forest_Change_dataset)

The forests of Sabah host a diverse array of plant and animal species. Most of Sabah's biodiversity is located in the [forest reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_reserve) areas, which formed half of its total landmass of 7.34 million hectares.[^cite_note-biodiversity-219] Its forest reserve are part of the 20 million hectares equatorial rainforests demarcated under the "[Heart of Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heart_of_Borneo)" initiative.[^cite_note-biodiversity-219] The forests surrounding the river valley of Kinabatangan River is the largest forest-covered floodplain in Malaysia.[^cite_note-220] The [Crocker Range National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocker_Range_National_Park) is the largest [national park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_park) in the state, covering an area of 139,919 hectares. Most of the park area are covered in dense forest and important as a water catchment area with its [headwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_source) connecting to five major rivers in the west coast area.[^cite_note-221] [Kinabalu National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinabalu_National_Park) was inscribed as a [World Heritage Site](44940.md) by UNESCO in 2000 for its richness in plant diversity combined with its unique geological, topographical, and climatic conditions.[^cite_note-222] The park hosts more than 4,500 species of flora and fauna, including 326 bird and around 100 mammal species along with over 110 land snail species.[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-224]



A walkout through the [Sepilok Orangutan Rehabilitation Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepilok_Orangutan_Rehabilitation_Centre)

[Tiga Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiga_Island,_Malaysia) is formed through the eruption of [mud volcano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mud_volcano) in 1897. The island is now part of the [Tiga Island National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiga_Island_National_Park) together with [Kalampunian Besar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalampunian_Besar_Island) and [Kalampunian Damit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalampunian_Damit_Island) islands as a tourist attractions,[^cite_note-225] with a [mud bath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mud_bath) tourism.[^cite_note-226] The [Tunku Abdul Rahman National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunku_Abdul_Rahman_National_Park) is a group of five islands of [Gaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaya_Island), [Manukan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manukan_Island), Mamutik, Sapi and [Sulug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulug_Island). These islands are believed to once connected to the Crocker Range but separated when sea levels rose since the last [ice age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_age).[^cite_note-227] The [Tun Mustapha Marine Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tun_Mustapha_Marine_Park) is the largest [marine park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_park) located in the north of Sabah. It covers the three major islands of [Banggi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banggi_Island), [Balambangan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balambangan_Island) and [Malawali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malawali_Island).[^cite_note-228] Another marine park is the [Tun Sakaran Marine Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tun_Sakaran_Marine_Park) located in the south-east of Sabah. The park comprising the islands of [Bodgaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodgaya_Island), [Boheydulang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boheydulang_Island), Sabangkat and Salakan along with sand [cays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cay) of Maiga, Mantabuan and Sibuan. Bodgaya is gazetted as a forest reserve, while Boheydulang as a [bird sanctuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird_sanctuary).[^cite_note-229] These islands are formed by Quaternary pyroclastic material that was ejected during explosive volcanic activities.[^cite_note-230]



[Coral reefs](87410.md) in [Tunku Abdul Rahman National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunku_Abdul_Rahman_National_Park)

The [Tawau Hills National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawau_Hills_National_Park) established as a natural [water catchment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_catchment) area. The park contains rugged volcanic landscapes including a [hot spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_spring) and spectacular waterfalls. Bordering the Philippine Turtle Islands is the [Turtle Islands National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turtle_Islands_National_Park_\(Malaysia\)), it consists of three islands of Selingaan, Bakkungan Kechil and Gulisaan which is notable as the nesting place for [green turtle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_turtle) and [hawksbill sea turtle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawksbill_sea_turtle).[^cite_note-231] Other important wildlife regions in Sabah include the [Maliau Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maliau_Basin), [Danum Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danum_Valley), [Tabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabin), Imbak Canyon and [Sepilok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepilok). These places are either designated as national parks, wildlife reserves, virgin jungle reserves, or protection forest reserve. Beyond the coasts of Sabah lie a number of islands rich with [coral reefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coral_reefs) such as Ligitan, Sipadan, Selingaan, Tiga and [Layang-Layang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Layang-Layang_Island) (Swallow Reef). Other main islands including the [Jambongan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jambongan_Island), [Timbun Mata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timbun_Mata_Island), Bum Bum and the divided [Sebatik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebatik_Island). The Sabah state government has enacted several laws to protect its forests and endangered wildlife species under the Animals Ordinance 1962,[^cite_note-232] Forest Enactment 1968[^cite_note-233] and the [Wildlife Conservation Enactment 1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_Conservation_Enactment_1997)[^cite_note-wildlife_enactment-234] among others.[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-forestry_laws-236] Under the Wildlife Conservation Enactment, any persons hunting inside conservation lands are liable for imprisonment for five years and fined with [RM](360278.md)50,000.[^cite_note-wildlife_enactment-234] The state government also plans to implement seasonal huntings as part of its conservation efforts to prevent the continuous lose of its endangered wildlife species while maintaining the state indigenous hunting traditions.[^cite_note-237]

#### Conservation issues



A lorry carrying timber in Tawau. Logging has contributed over 50% of the state revenue.

[^cite_note-logging-238]

Since the post-World War II timber boom driven by the need of raw materials from [industrial countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country), Sabah forests have been gradually eroded by uncontrolled timber exploitation and the conversion of Sabah forest lands into [palm oil](57561.md) plantations.[^cite_note-239] Since 1970, forestry sector have contributed for over 50% of the state revenue, of which a study conducted in 1997 revealed the state had almost depleted all of its [virgin forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old-growth_forest) outside the conservation areas.[^cite_note-logging-238] The state government were determined to maintain the state biodiversity while to make sure the state economy continue to alive.[^cite_note-240] While in the same time facing hard task to control such activities although there is laws to prevent it.[^cite_note-forestry_laws-236] The need for development and basic necessities also became an issue while to preserving the nature.[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242] Mining activities had directly released [pollutants of heavy metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toxic_heavy_metal) into rivers, reservoirs, ponds and affecting groundwater through the leaching of mine [tailings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tailings). An environmental report released in 1994 reported the presence of heavy metal at the Damit/Tuaran River that exceeded the water quality safe levels for consumption. The water in Liwagu River also reported the presence of heavy metal which was believed to be originated from the [Mamut Mine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamut_Mine).[^cite_note-env-243] [Forest fire](56106.md) also have become the latest concern due to drought and fires set by irresponsible farmers or individuals such as what happened in the [2016 forest fires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Malaysian_haze), where thousands of hectares of forest reserves in [Binsuluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binsuluk_Forest_Reserve) on the west coast of Sabah were lost.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245]



Aerial view of [Mamut Mine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamut_Mine) with water that has filled the mine. Its water is reported to be dangerous for consumption due to the high presence of [heavy metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toxic_heavy_metal).

Rampant [fish bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blast_fishing) has destroyed many coral reefs and affecting fisheries production in the state.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247] Moreover, the illegal activities of the extraction of river sand and gravel in the rivers of Padas, Papar and Tuaran had become the latest concern along with the wildlife and marine hunting and [poaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poaching).[^cite_note-env-243] Due to severe deforestation along with massive wildlife and marine poaching, the [Sumatran rhino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumatran_rhinoceros) have been declared as extinct in early 2015.[^cite_note-248] Some other species that was threatened with extinction is [banteng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banteng),[^cite_note-249] [bearded pig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bornean_bearded_pig),[^cite_note-wpd-250] [clouded leopard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clouded_leopard), [dugong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dugong),[^cite_note-251] [elephant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borneo_elephant), [false gharial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_gharial), green turtle, hawksbill sea turtle, [orangutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bornean_orangutan), [pangolin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunda_pangolin),[^cite_note-252] [proboscis monkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proboscis_monkey),[^cite_note-253] [river shark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borneo_river_shark),[^cite_note-sharks_and_stingray-254] [roughnose stingray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roughnose_stingray),[^cite_note-sharks_and_stingray-254] [sambar deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambar_deer), [shark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borneo_shark) and [sun bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun_bear).[^cite_note-wpd-250][^cite_note-255] Although the indigenous community are also involved in hunting, they hunt based on their [spiritual believes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritualism_\(beliefs\)) and practice, and on a small scale, which differentiates them from poachers.[^cite_note-256] Well-known indigenous practices, such as "*maganu totuo*" or "*montok kosukopan*", "*tuwa di powigian*", "*managal*" or "*tagal*" and "*meminting*", have helped to maintain resources and prevent their depletion.[^cite_note-257]

## Economy



Sabah's [Desa Dairy Farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desa_Dairy_Farm), dubbed as the little [New Zealand](4913064.md) due to the similar scenery view with the latter country

[^cite_note-258]

[^cite_note-259]

Sabah GDP Share by Sector (2016)

[^cite_note-260]

1. Services (40.8%)

2. Mining & Quarrying (29.6%)

3. Agriculture (19.1%)

4. Manufacturing (7.50%)

5. Construction (2.60%)

Sabah's economy is mainly based on [primary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_sector_of_the_economy) such as agriculture, forestry and [petroleum](994887.md).[^cite_note-sbh-2][^cite_note-eco-261] Currently, the [tertiary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector_of_the_economy) plays an important part to the state economy, especially in tourism and services. With its richness in biodiversity, the state is offering [ecotourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecotourism). Although in recent years the tourism industry has been affected by [attacks and kidnapping of tourists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross_border_attacks_in_Sabah) by militant groups based in the southern Philippines, it remained stable with the increase of security in eastern Sabah and the Sulu Sea.[^cite_note-262] The tourism sector contribute 10% share of the state [Gross domestic product](12594.md) (GDP) and was predicted to increase more.[^cite_note-263] Majority of the tourists come from China (60.3%), followed by [South Korea](27019.md) (33.9%), Australia (16.3%) and [Taiwan](25734.md) (8.3%).[^cite_note-264] Tourism plays a crucial role in the state's economy as the third largest income generating sectors with the state itself recorded a total of 3,879,413 tourist arrivals in 2018, a growth of 5.3% compared to 3,684,734 in 2017.[^cite_note-265] Since the 1950s, [rubber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_rubber) and [copra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copra) are the main source of agricultural economy of North Borneo.[^cite_note-266] The timber industry started to emerged in the 1960s due to high demand of raw materials from industrial countries. This was however replaced by petroleum in the 1970s after the discovery of oil in the area of west coast Sabah.[^cite_note-267] In the same year, [cocoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocoa_bean) and [palm oil](57561.md) was added to the list.[^cite_note-eco-261][^cite_note-268] The Sabah state government managed to increase the state fund from RM6 million to RM12 billion and poverty was down by almost half to 33.1% in 1980.[^cite_note-Lim2008-81] The state rapid development on primary sector has attracted those [job seekers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Job_hunting) in neighbouring Indonesia and the Philippines as the state labour force itself are not sufficient.[^cite_note-269] The state GDP at the time ranked behind [Selangor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selangor) and [Kuala Lumpur](16854.md), being the third richest although the manufacturing sector remained small.[^cite_note-env-243][^cite_note-270] However, by 2000, the state started to become the poorest as it still dependent on natural resources as its primary sources of income comparing to those [secondary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_sector_of_the_economy) producer states.[^cite_note-271] Thus the [Sabah Development Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Development_Corridor) (SDC) was established in 2008 by Prime Minister [Abdullah Ahmad Badawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Ahmad_Badawi) with a total investment of RM105 billion for 18 years to increase the state GDP to RM63.2 billion by 2025.[^cite_note-sdc-272] Around RM5.83 billion were allocated each year for infrastructures development along with the creation of 900,000 jobs.[^cite_note-sdc-272] The federal government targeted to eradicate hardcore poverty by the end [Ninth Malaysia Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninth_Malaysia_Plan) (9MP) with overall poverty halved from 23% in 2004 to 12% in 2010 and 8.1% in 2012.[^cite_note-sdc-272] Since its establishment in 2008, the state GDP increase to 10.7% which was higher than the national economic growth of 4.8% and the world economic growth of 2.7%. Following the [world financial crisis in 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_financial_crisis_in_2009), Sabah GDP recorded 4.8% growth compared to −1.5% for national level and −0.4% for world level.[^cite_note-sdc-272]



[Paddy field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paddy_field) in [Tambunan District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tambunan_District)

From 2010 to 2011, the state experienced a slower growth due to weaker performance on the oil and gas sector. Based on 2014 survey, Sabah GDP recorded a 5.0% growth and remained as the largest contributor in agriculture sector with 18.1%, followed by [Sarawak](28258.md), Johor, [Pahang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahang) and [Perak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perak). Its [GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_per_capita) however are still lowest with RM19,672, the third lowest after [Kelantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelantan) (RM11,815) and [Kedah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kedah) (RM17,321) from all 13 states.[^cite_note-GDP-273] In the same year, the state export value stood at RM45.3 billion with an import value of RM36.5 billion. Machinery and transportation equipment accounted for most of the imported products followed by fuel, mineral lubricants and others. While Sabah mostly exports raw petroleum and palm oil.[^cite_note-274] The state currently has a total of eight ports with two in [Sepanggar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepanggar) while each one in Kota Kinabalu, Sandakan, Tawau, Kudat, [Kunak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunak) and Lahad Datu that was operated and maintained by the Sabah Ports Authority owned by Suria Group.[^cite_note-sabah_ports-275] As part of the [Eleventh Malaysia Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleventh_Malaysia_Plan) (11MP), the federal government has approved an allocation of RM800 million to expand the cargo handling of Sapangar Bay Container Port from 500,000 to 1.25 million [TEUs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-foot_equivalent_unit) per annum as well to accommodate larger ship like [Panamax](504868.md)-size vessels.[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277] An additional allocation of RM333.51 million was given in the same year, making it a total of RM1.13 billion with the project will start in 2017.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279] The fisheries industries remain the important part of Sabah primary sector economy with a contribution for about 200,000 metric tonnes of fish worth RM700 annually as well contributing 2.8% to the state annual GDP.[^cite_note-fishdept-199] While the [aquaculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquaculture) and marine fish cage sector have produce 35,000 metric tons of [brackish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brackish_water) and [fresh waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fresh_water) aquaculture and 360 metric ton of [groupers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grouper), [wrasses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrasse), snappers and [lobsters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobster) worth around RM60 million and RM13 million respectively. Sabah is also one of the producer of [seaweed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seaweed), with most of the farms are located in the seas around [Semporna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semporna).[^cite_note-fishdept-199] Although recently the seaweed industry was heavily affected by spate of kidnappings perpetrated by the southern-Philippine-based [Abu Sayyaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Sayyaf) militant group.[^cite_note-280]



Fishery activities in the harbour of [Sandakan](1732138.md)

As of 2015, Sabah was producing 180,000 [barrel of oil equivalent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barrel_of_oil_equivalent) per day[^cite_note-281] and currently receives 5% oil royalty (percentage of oil production paid by the mining company to the lease owner) from Petronas over oil explorations in Sabah territorial waters based on the 1974 Petroleum Development Act.[^cite_note-Lim2008-81][^cite_note-oxford_review-282] Majority of the oil and gas deposits are located on Sabah Trough basin in the west coast side.[^cite_note-283] Sabah was also given a 10% stake in Petronas [liquefied natural gas](832128.md) (LNG) in [Bintulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bintulu), Sarawak.[^cite_note-284] Income inequality and the high cost living remain the major economic issues in Sabah.[^cite_note-285] The high cost living has been blamed on the Cabotage Policy, although the cause was due to the smaller trade volumes, cost of transport and efficiency of port to handle trade.[^cite_note-286] The government has set to review the Cabotage Policy even thought the cause was due to other reasons with the World Bank has stated that the result was due to weak distribution channels, high handling charges and inefficient inland transportation.[^cite_note-287] It was finally agreed to exempt the policy from 1 June 2017; with foreign ships will go directly to ports in the East without need to go to West Malaysia although Cabotage Policy on transshipment of goods within Sabah and Sarawak and the federal territory of Labuan remain.[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] Prime Minister Najib also promised to narrow development gap between Sabah and the [Peninsular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_Malaysia) by improving and built more infrastructures in the state,[^cite_note-promised-290] in which it was continued under the [Pakatan Harapan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakatan_Harapan) (PH) administration where the new federal government also said the state should develop in par with Peninsular with the federal government will be consistent in commitment to helping develop the state as stated by Deputy Prime Minister [Wan Azizah Wan Ismail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wan_Azizah_Wan_Ismail).[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292] Based on a latest record, the total unemployment in the state have been reduced from 5.1% (2014) to 4.7% (2015), although the number of unemployment was still high.[^cite_note-293] [Slum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slum) is almost non-existent in Malaysia but due to the [high number of refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_Philippines) arriving from the [troubling southern Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moro_conflict_in_the_Philippines), Sabah has since seen a significant rise on its numbers. To eliminate [water pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_pollution) and improve a better [hygiene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hygiene), the Sabah state government are working to relocate them into a better housing settlement.[^cite_note-294] As part of the [BIMP-EAGA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BIMP-EAGA), Sabah also continued to position itself as a main gateway for regional investments. Foreign investment are mainly concentrated in the Kota Kinabalu Industrial Park (KKIP) areas.[^cite_note-oxford_review-282] Although country such as Japan have mainly focusing their various development and investment projects in the interior and islands since after the end of Second World War.[^cite_note-295] Following America's abandonment in the [Trans-Pacific Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Pacific_Partnership) (TPPA) economic agreements in early 2017, Sabah began to turns its trade to China and [India](14533.md) markets.[^cite_note-296] To further accelerate its economic growth, Sabah also targets several more countries as its main trade partners including [Germany](11867.md), South Korea, Thailand and the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) as the destinations of exports for [food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food)-based products, Brunei, Indonesia, Taiwan, the United States and New Zealand as the destinations for palm oil and [logistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logistics) sector, [Russia](25391.md) as the destination for the oil and gas industry and Japan and Vietnam as the destinations for the [wood-based furniture industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood_industry).[^cite_note-297]

## Infrastructure

Sabah's public infrastructure are still lagged behind mostly due to its geographical challenges as the second largest state in Malaysia.[^cite_note-Group-19][^cite_note-infrastructure-298] The Sabah Ministry of Infrastructure Development (formerly known as Ministry of Communication and Works) is responsible for all public infrastructure planning and development in the state.[^cite_note-299] To narrow the development gap, the federal government are working to build more infrastructures and improve the already available one.[^cite_note-promised-290] In 2013, Sabah state government allocates RM1.583 billion for infrastructure and public facilities development,[^cite_note-300] of which the state were allocated another RM4.07 billion by the federal government in 2015 Malaysian Budget.[^cite_note-301] Since the Eight Malaysia Plan (8MP) until 2014, a total of RM11.115 billion has been allocated for various infrastructure projects in the state.[^cite_note-302] Under the [Tenth Malaysia Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenth_Malaysia_Plan) (10MP), infrastructure in the rural areas was given attention with the increase of rural water, electricity supply and road coverage.[^cite_note-303] Further large infrastructure allocation were delivered to both Sabah and Sarawak under the 2020 Malaysian Budget which include budget on improving connectivity and developing digital infrastructures for high speed internet in the rural areas.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305]

### Energy and water resources



High voltage [electricity pylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transmission_tower) located near the Kimanis Power Plant

Electricity distribution in the state as well in the [Federal Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Territory_\(Malaysia\)) of [Labuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labuan) are operated and managed by the [Sabah Electricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Electricity) [Sdn. Bhd.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_company_limited_by_shares) (SESB). Sabah electrics are mostly generated from [diesel power plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diesel_generator), [hydropower](14073.md) and [combined cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_cycle) power plants. The only main [hydroelectric plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric_plant) is the [Tenom Pangi Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenom_Pangi_Dam).[^cite_note-infrastructure-298] The combined cycle power plant called Kimanis Power Plant was completed in 2014, supplying 300 [MW](21347693.md), with 285 MW nominal capacity.[^cite_note-kpp-306] The plant is a joint venture between Petronas and NRG Consortium that also includes facilities such as [gas pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pipeline_transport) of [Sabah–Sarawak Gas Pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah%E2%80%93Sarawak_Gas_Pipeline) and a terminal of [Sabah Oil and Gas Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Oil_and_Gas_Terminal).[^cite_note-kpp-306] There is another two combined cycle power plants with a capacity of 380 MW operated by [Ranhill Holdings Berhad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranhill_Holdings_Berhad).[^cite_note-307] In 2009, the electricity coverage covers 67% of the state population and by 2011 increase to 80%.[^cite_note-infrastructure-298] The coverage reach 100% in 2012 after an allocation of RM962.5 million from the federal government were given to expand the coverage under the 2012 [National Budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_federal_budget).[^cite_note-308] The [electrical grid](20344155.md) is divided into two of West Coast and East Coast which has been integrated since 2007.[^cite_note-infrastructure-298] The West Coast Grid supplies electricity to Kota Kinabalu, Papar, Beaufort, Keningau, Kota Belud, Kota Marudu, Kudat and Labuan with a capacity of 488.4 MW and maximum demand of 396.5 MW.[^cite_note-infrastructure-298] While the East Coast Grid supplies electricity to the major towns of Sandakan, Kinabatangan, Lahad Datu, Kunak, Semporna and Tawau with a capacity of 333.02 MW and maximum demand of 203.3 MW.[^cite_note-infrastructure-298]

In 2018, the federal government upgraded the electrical grid of Sabah to reduce power interruption.[^cite_note-309] Neighbouring Sarawak also provide additional electricity power to Sabah in 2025.[^cite_note-310] [Electricity interconnection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_interconnection) between Sabah, the Indonesian province of [North Kalimantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Kalimantan) and the Philippine province of [Palawan](240395.md) as well for the whole [Mindanao](20546.md) islands are also in the process as part of the [BIMP-EAGA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BIMP-EAGA) and Borneo-Mindanao power interconnection under the [Association of Southeast Asian Nations](106539.md) (ASEAN) Power Grid;[^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313] with the interconnection with Palawan is expected to be commenced in the nearest future.[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316] Since 2007, there is an attempt to establish a [coal power plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil-fuel_power_station) in Lahad Datu which receiving opposition from local residents and non-governmental organisations for the pollution that would be caused by the plant.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318] Thus Sabah has start to exploring alternative ways to generate electricity with the usage of [renewable energy](25784.md) such as [solar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_energy), [mini hydro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_hydro), [biomass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomass), [geothermal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geothermal_energy) and micro-algae and [tidal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_power) technologies.[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320] The [Japanese government](30858300.md) has extended aid totalling RM172,190.93 for the solar electrification project in the island of [Larapan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larapan_Island) in Sabah's east coast in 2010.[^cite_note-321] In 2016, a research by United States GeothermEx Inc. and Jacobs New Zealand indicated the existence of an active geothermal system centred around the flanks of Mount Maria on Apas Kiri where it is suitable for Malaysia's first geothermal plant.[^cite_note-322] The construction for the first geothermal plant that expected to be completed in 2017 however was abandoned by the previous government in the mid-2016 with no sign of further progress.[^cite_note-323] A South Korean company [GS Caltex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GS_Caltex) also sets to build Malaysia's first [bio-butanol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bio-butanol) plant in the state.[^cite_note-324]



Babagon Dam, the biggest water catchment in the state

Piped [water supply](684928.md) in the state is managed by the Sabah State Water Department, an agency under the control of Sabah Ministry of Infrastructure Development. Operating with 73 water treatments plants, an average of 1.19 billion litres of water are distributed daily to meet Sabahan residents demands.[^cite_note-water_supply-325] The coverage of water supply in major towns has reach 100% while in rural areas, the coverage still around 75% with total public pipes length up to 15,031 kilometres.[^cite_note-water_supply-325] Some communities use [gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravity_of_earth?action=edit\&redlink=1) water systems.[^cite_note-326] The only water supply dam in the state is the Babagon Dam which holds 21,000 million litres of water.[^cite_note-327] To meet the increase demands, another dam named as Kaiduan Dam was being proposed to be built although being met with protest from local villagers who living on the proposed site.[^cite_note-328] Sabah has a natural gas demand of 9.9 million [cubic metres](30125638.md) (350 million [cubic feet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubic_foot)) per day at [standard conditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_temperature_and_pressure) in 2013, which increase to 14.8 million m<sup>3</sup> (523 million ft<sup>3</sup>) per day in 2015.[^cite_note-329] As Malaysia's [liquefied petroleum gas](361384.md) (LPG) are much cheaper through the [subsidy](146719.md) that was given by the federal government, it was found out in 2015 that around 20,000 LPG cylinders in Sabah east coast were [smuggled](28645.md) by immigrants from neighbouring Indonesia and the southern Philippines in a monthly basis to their countries that leading to many Sabahans hard to retrieve enough supplies of LPG.[^cite_note-330] As a counter-measure, the [Malaysian Ministry of Domestic Trade, Co-operatives and Consumerism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Domestic_Trade,_Co-operatives_and_Consumerism_\(Malaysia\)) (MDTCAC) has temporarily cancelled all permits to sell gas cylinders into neighbouring countries with a new policy will be implemented to control such illegal activities.[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332]

### Telecommunication and broadcasting



Telecommunication towers atop Mount Silam facing [Darvel Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darvel_Bay) of Lahad Datu

Telecommunication in Sabah and Sarawak were originally administered by Posts and Telecommunication Department until 1967,[^cite_note-telecom-333] and maintained by the British [Cable & Wireless Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cable_&_Wireless_Communications) before all telecommunications management in the state been takeover by Peninsular-based company.[^cite_note-changing_role-334] The British telecommunication company have establish a [submarine cable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Submarine_communications_cable) that linking Kota Kinabalu with Singapore and [Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Hong_Kong).[^cite_note-changing_role-334] Following the expansion of the Peninsular-based company on 1 January 1968, Sabah Posts and Telecommunication Department was merged with the Peninsular telecommunication department to form Telecommunications Department Malaysia. All operations under Telecommunications Department Malaysia was then transferred to Syarikat [Telekom Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telekom_Malaysia) Berhad (STM) which become a public listed company in 1991 with the federal government retained a majority shareholding.[^cite_note-telecom-333] There are also other telecommunication companies operating in the state although only providing cellular phone facilities. In 2006, the state has the lowest Direct Exchange Line (DEL) penetration rate, with cellular and internet dial-up penetrations rate only 6.5 per 100 inhabitants.[^cite_note-infrastructure-298] Most residents from the low income groups would rather use mobile phones internet or use internet at their offices instead of setting up internet access at home due to the expensive cost and slow services.[^cite_note-infrastructure-298] Until the end of 2014, there were only 934 telecommunication hotspots in Sabah.[^cite_note-335] Due to this, the government are working to increase the penetration and capability of internet connection as well to bridge the gap between Sabah and the Peninsular.[^cite_note-336] From 2016, [Unifi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unifi_\(internet_service_provider\)) [fibre optic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optical_fibre) coverage began to expand to other towns aside from the main city and major towns,[^cite_note-337] alongside [Celcom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celcom) and [Maxis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxis_Communications) by the following year with a speed up to 100 [Mbit/s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mbit/s).[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339] In 2019, [Digi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digi_Telecommunications) launches its home fibre broadband in Sabah with speed up to 1 [Gbit/s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gbit/s).[^cite_note-340] The mobile telecommunications in Sabah are mostly use [5G](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5G) and [4G](486547.md) with the state internet being upgraded by the Malaysia's federal government through the *Pelan Jalinan Digital Negara* (JENDELA) initiative.[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342] In 2025, [U Mobile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U_Mobile) expanded their 5G network in [Kota Kinabalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kota_Kinabalu) through the ULTRA5G in conjunction with the celebration of both Sabah Day and Malaysia's National Day.[^cite_note-343]

The previous state internet traffic are routed through a hub in Malaysia's capital of Kuala Lumpur, passing through a submarine cable connecting the Peninsular with Kota Kinabalu. The systems are considered as costly and inefficient especially due to the price of leasing [bandwidth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandwidth_\(computing\)) with the large distance.[^cite_note-Group-19] In 2000, there is a plan to establish Sabah own internet hub but the plan was unreachable due to the high cost and low usage rates in the state. Other alternative plan including using the Brunei internet [gateway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gateway_\(telecommunications\)) in a short term before establishing Sabah own gateway.[^cite_note-Group-19] By 2016, the federal government has start to establish the first internet gateway for East Malaysia with the laying of 60 [terabyte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terabyte) submarine cable which are developed by a private company named Xiddig Cellular Communications Sdn. Bhd. at a cost of about RM850 million through the [Private Funding Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_finance_initiative) (PFI).[^cite_note-344] Under the 2015 Malaysian Budget project of 1Malaysia Cable System Project (SKR1M), a new submarine cable for high speed internet has been built from Kota Kinabalu to [Pahang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahang) in the Peninsular which completed in 2017.[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346] The 1Malaysia submarine cable system linking the state capital with [Miri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miri,_Malaysia), [Bintulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bintulu) and [Kuching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuching) in Sarawak together with [Mersing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mersing) in Johor with an increase of bandwidth capacity up to 12 [terabyte per second](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terabyte_per_second).[^cite_note-347] Another submarine cable, the BIMP-EAGA Submarine and Terrestrial (BEST) Cable Project is currently being built from Kota Kinabalu to Tawau to connecting Sabah with Brunei, Kalimantan and Mindanao which will be completed in 2018.[^cite_note-348] In early 2016, a [Memorandum of Understanding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorandum_of_Understanding) (MoU) was signed between the state government and China's largest networking company, [Huawei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huawei) to set Sabah to become [information and communications technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_and_communications_technology) (ICT) hub by leveraging on Huawei's ICT expertise.[^cite_note-349] More free high speed Wi-Fi hotspots are being planned in Sabah, especially to the state capital.[^cite_note-350]



The building of Ministry of Communications and Multimedia Integrated Complex in [Donggongon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donggongon) - the main office of Sabah RTM

Sabah launched its radio service on 9 November 1955, which became a part of Radio Malaysia when it joined Malaysia in 1963 and later part of the bigger [Radio Televisyen Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Televisyen_Malaysia) (RTM) in 1969, when the nation's radio and television operations merged.[^cite_note-351] On 28 December 1971, RTM launched a third TV station solely for Sabah, known as [Rangkaian Ketiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rangkaian_Ketiga) or Network Three. But following the construction of earth satellite station near [Kuantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuantan), [Pahang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahang) and [Kinarut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinarut) for communications and television broadcast via the [Indian Ocean Intelsat III satellite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelsat) and the introduction of TV1 on 30 August 1975 and TV2 on 31 August 1983 in the state, it ceased to air by mid-1985. RTM has four branches in the state - a main office in capital city Kota Kinabalu and three other offices in Keningau, Sandakan and Tawau. The main office produces news and shows for RTM's television channels and operates two state radio channels, namely Sabah FM and Sabah V FM, whereas three other offices operate district radio channels such as Keningau FM, Sandakan FM and Tawau FM.

Other radio channels in the state include KK FM which is operated by [Universiti Malaysia Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universiti_Malaysia_Sabah),[^cite_note-352] and Bayu FM which is only available through [Astro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astro_\(Malaysian_satellite_television\)), the Malaysian main satellite television.[^cite_note-353] Several newly independent radio station have recently been launched in the state, namely [Kupi-Kupi FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kupi-Kupi_FM) in 2016,[^cite_note-354] [KK12FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KK12FM) and [VOKFM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VOKFM) in 2017.[^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356] Other Peninsular-based radio stations also had set up their offices in the state to tap the emerging market. Sabahan DJs are mostly hired and local state songs will be played to meet Sabahan listeners taste and slang. Television broadcasting in the state is divided into [terrestrial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrestrial_television) and satellite television. As Malaysia aims for [digital television transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_television_transition), all [analogue signal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analog_television) will be shut down soon.[^cite_note-357] There are two types of [free-to-air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free-to-air) television provider such as [MYTV Broadcasting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MYTV_Broadcasting) (digital terrestrial) and [Astro NJOI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astro_NJOI) (satellite). On the other hand, [IPTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPTV) is available via the [Unifi TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unifi_TV) through Unifi fibre optic internet subscription.[^cite_note-358] The state first established newspaper is the Sabah Times (rebranded as the [New Sabah Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Sabah_Times)), founded by Fuad Stephens, who became the first Chief Minister of Sabah.[^cite_note-359] Other main newspapers include the independent [Daily Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daily_Express_\(Malaysia\)),[^cite_note-360] [Overseas Chinese Daily News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Chinese_Daily_News),[^cite_note-361] the Sarawak-based [The Borneo Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Borneo_Post),[^cite_note-362] the Peninsular-based [Sin Chew Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sin_Chew_Daily)[^cite_note-363] and the Brunei-based [Borneo Bulletin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borneo_Bulletin).[^cite_note-364]

### Transportation



[Eight-lane highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controlled-access_highway) in the capital city of Kota Kinabalu, part of the [Pan-Borneo Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Borneo_Highway)

Sabah has a total of 21,934 kilometres (13,629 mi) road network in 2016, of which 11,355 kilometres (7,056 mi) are [sealed road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sealed_road).[^cite_note-365] Before the formation of Malaysia, the state together with Sarawak only has rudimentary road systems.[^cite_note-366] Most [trunk roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trunk_road) was then constructed from the 1970s until the 1980s under the [World Bank](36752.md) loans. In 2005, 61% of road coverage in the state were still [gravel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravel_road) and unpaved, comprising 1,428 kilometres (887 mi) [federal roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Federal_Roads_system) and 14,249 kilometres (8,854 mi) [state roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_State_Roads_system), of which 6,094 kilometres (3,787 mi) are sealed while the remaining 9,583 kilometres (5,955 mi) were gravel and unpaved roads.[^cite_note-infrastructure-298] This led to disparity between roads in the state with those in the Peninsular, with only 38.9% are sealed while 89.4% have been sealed in the Peninsular. Due to this, SDC was implemented to expand the road coverage in Sabah along with the construction of [Pan-Borneo Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Borneo_Highway). Since the 9MP, various road projects has been undertaken under the SDC and around RM50 million has been spent to repairs Sabah main roads since the 8MP.[^cite_note-infrastructure-298] The high cost to repair roads frequently has led the Sabah state government to find other alternative ways to connecting every major districts by tunnelling roads through highlands which will also saving time and fuel as the distance being shortened as well to bypass landslides.[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368] In early 2016, the expansion project of Pan-Borneo Highway has been launched to expand the road size from [single carriageway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single_carriageway) to four-lane road, while city highway been expand from four-lane to eight-lane as well with the construction of new routes which will connect the state with Sarawak, Brunei and the [Trans Kalimantan Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans_Kalimantan_Highway) in Indonesia.[^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370] The project is divided into two packages: the first package covering the West Coast area will complete in 2021, while the second covering the East Coast area will finish in 2022.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372][^cite_note-pan_borneo-373] All state roads are maintained under the state's Public Works Department,[^cite_note-374] while federal roads maintained by the national [Public Works Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Public_Works_Department).[^cite_note-375]



[Sabah State Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_State_Railway) train passing through a tunnel of the [Western Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Sabah_Railway_Line) in Pengalat Besar, [Papar District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papar_District)



Boats and ferries at the Kota Kinabalu marina

Sabah uses a [dual carriageway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual_carriageway) with the [left-hand traffic rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-_and_right-hand_traffic).[^cite_note-pan_borneo-373][^cite_note-Thiessen2012-376] All major towns in Sabah provide public transportation services such as buses, taxis and vans along with [Grab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grab_\(company\)) services. The [KK Sentral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KK_Sentral) operating express [bus](4146.md) services from the city to [Beaufort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaufort,_Malaysia), [Sipitang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sipitang), [Menumbok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menumbok), [Lawas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawas) and [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei).[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] The [BRT Kota Kinabalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BRT_Kota_Kinabalu) is currently under construction to provide [bus rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_rapid_transit) (BRT) system in [Kota Kinabalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kota_Kinabalu).[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380] A rail transport through the [Western Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Sabah_Railway_Line) operated by the [Sabah State Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_State_Railway) provides daily services for commuters, travellers, as well as for cargo transportation. A separate company owned by Sutera Harbour known as the North Borneo Railway operates leisure tour for tourists.[^cite_note-381] The train station and terminal is located in [Tanjung Aru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanjung_Aru_railway_station), not far from the city airport.[^cite_note-382] Other main stations including in [Papar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papar_railway_station_\(Malaysia\)), [Beaufort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaufort_railway_station,_Sabah) and [Tenom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenom_railway_station). The current [Aeropod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeropod) projects on the main station in Tanjung Aru will modernise the station and provide a provision for future [light rail transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail) (LRT).[^cite_note-383] In early 2016, the state government has purchased a new [diesel multiple unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diesel_multiple_unit) (DMU) for about RM8 million to replace the old train used between Beaufort and Tenom while the rail line from Halogilat and Tenom will be upgrading by the federal government at the cost of RM99.5 million along with the arrival of another three DMUs that will be received in early 2018.[^cite_note-384] [Kota Kinabalu International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kota_Kinabalu_International_Airport) is the main gateway to Sabah.[^cite_note-KKIA-385] In 2005, the Malaysian federal government approved major renovation and refurbishment works to the main terminal (Terminal 1) as well as a runway expansion with construction began in 2006.[^cite_note-386] As a result of the expansion, the airport is able to accommodate large passenger aircraft such as the [Boeing 747](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_747).[^cite_note-KKIA-385] It has also become the second busiest airport in [Malaysia](3607937.md), after [Kuala Lumpur International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuala_Lumpur_International_Airport) (KLIA) in West Malaysia.[^cite_note-KKIA-385] In 2018, the [Malaysia Airlines](153162.md) conducts test flight for its new long-range capability passenger aircraft of [Airbus A350](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_A350) to the airport from Kuala Lumpur as a replacement to the largest aircraft of [Airbus A380](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_A380) since it is too large for Malaysian aviation markets.[^cite_note-387] Other smaller airports in Sabah including [Kudat Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kudat_Airport), [Lahad Datu Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahad_Datu_Airport), [Sandakan Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandakan_Airport) and [Tawau Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawau_Airport). [Layang-Layang Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Layang-Layang_Airport) in [Swallow Reef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swallow_Reef) served as a military and civilian airport.[^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389] Three airlines fly from Peninsular Malaysia to Sabah: Malaysia Airlines, [AirAsia](285974.md), and [Malindo Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malindo_Air).[^cite_note-390] [Sabah Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Air) is a helicopter [chartered flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_charter) company owned by the Sabah state government, serving flights for aerial sightseeing to interested customers as well for the transportation of state government servants.[^cite_note-391]



[Kota Kinabalu International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kota_Kinabalu_International_Airport) (Terminal 1)

Sabah has a total of eight ports operating in Sepanggar, Kota Kinabalu, Sandakan, Tawau, Kudat, Kunak and Lahad Datu.[^cite_note-sabah_ports-275] The Sapangar Bay Container Port is the main [transshipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transshipment) hub for the BIMP-EAGA region. Another port, the Sapangar Bay Oil Terminal is the main terminal for refined petroleum products and liquid chemical in the West Coast. Kota Kinabalu Port remain as a general cargo port. While all ports in the northern and eastern Sabah served to handle palm oil related products such as fertiliser, [palm kernel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm_kernel) as well for general cargo.[^cite_note-sabah_ports-275] Ferry service in the West Coast side provide trips to Labuan from the Jesselton Point Waterfront and [Menumbok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menumbok) Ferry Terminal in [Kuala Penyu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuala_Penyu).[^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393] In the East Coast, the service are provided from the Tawau Ferry Terminal to Nunukan and [Tarakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarakan,_North_Kalimantan) in Kalimantan, Indonesia.[^cite_note-394] There is also ferry services from Sandakan to [Zamboanga City](25167165.md) and a new one that was planned from Kudat to Buliluyan, [Bataraza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bataraza) of [Palawan](240395.md) in the Philippines, but both services were terminated at the moment due to lack of security enforcement from the Philippine side prior to the persistent attack by pirates and kidnapping by militant groups based in the Sulu Archipelago of the southern Philippines.[^cite_note-395][^cite_note-396] The planned ferry service from Kudat to Palawan was restored on 1 February 2017 after the increasing of security enforcement from the Philippines side,[^cite_note-economy_resumed-192] but were postponed again until present mainly due to both ferry operators from Malaysia and the Philippines facing difficulties in complying with the necessary requirements and permits imposed by both national and state authorities.[^cite_note-397]

### Healthcare

See also: [List of hospitals in Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Malaysia)



Gleneagles Kota Kinabalu, one of the main [private hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_hospital) in Sabah

Sabah has four major government hospitals: [Queen Elizabeth Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_Hospital,_Kota_Kinabalu), [Queen Elizabeth Hospital II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_Hospital_II), [Duchess of Kent Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchess_of_Kent_Hospital) and Tawau Hospital followed by 13 other government districts hospitals,[^cite_note-398] women and children hospital, mental hospital, public health clinics, and rural clinics. Besides government-owned hospitals and clinics, there are also a number of private hospitals such as: Gleneagles Kota Kinabalu, [KPJ Specialist Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KPJ_Sabah_Specialist_Hospital), Damai Specialist Centre (DSC), Rafflesia Specialist Centre (RSC) and Jesselton Medical Centre (JMC).[^cite_note-docs_exodus-399] There is also an addiction treatment facility known as [Solace Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solace_Sabah) in the state capital to treat problems related to alcoholism and [drug addiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Addiction).

In 2011, the state's doctor-patient ratio was 1\:2,480 – lower than the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) recommendation of 1 doctor to 600 patients.[^cite_note-400] Because of the heavy workload and lack of interest from younger graduates, Sabah is facing the shortage of doctors.[^cite_note-401] Many doctors who once served under the government hospitals have decided to move to private hospitals instead because of the heavy workload with low salaries in government hospitals although private hospitals won't easily recruiting them with some applications have been turned down.[^cite_note-docs_exodus-399] Thus to prevent the continuous shortage of doctors, the federal government has initiated various measure to produce more physicians with massive funds has been allocated to healthcare sector in every year country budget.[^cite_note-402]

### Education

Main articles: [List of schools in Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_schools_in_Sabah) and [List of universities in Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Malaysia)



[Universiti Malaysia Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universiti_Malaysia_Sabah) (UMS) chancellory building

All primary and secondary schools are under the jurisdiction and observation of the Sabah State Education Department, under the guidance of the national [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_\(Malaysia\)).[^cite_note-403] The oldest schools in Sabah are: [St. Michael's School Sandakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Michael's_Secondary_School) (1886), [St. Michael's School Penampang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SM_St._Michael,_Penampang) (1888), All Saints' School, Likas (1903) and St. Patrick's School Tawau (1917).[^cite_note-404] Based on 2013 statistics, Sabah has a total of 207 government secondary schools,[^cite_note-405] five [international schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_school) (comprising Charis International School,[^cite_note-406] Kinabalu International School,[^cite_note-407] Sayfol International School,[^cite_note-408] as well the Indonesian School of Kota Kinabalu[^cite_note-409] and Japanese School of Kota Kinabalu).[^cite_note-410] and nine [Chinese independent schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_independent_high_school). Sabah has a considerable number of indigenous students enrolled in Chinese schools.[^cite_note-bcs-411]

Sabah state government also emphasises pre-school education in the state. This was followed with the aid from [Sabah Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Foundation) (Yayasan Sabah) and [Nestlé](160227.md) who helped to establish pre-schools in the state.[^cite_note-412][^cite_note-413] Sabah has two public universities: [Universiti Malaysia Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universiti_Malaysia_Sabah) (UMS) and [Universiti Teknologi MARA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universiti_Teknologi_MARA) (UiTM). [Universiti Tun Abdul Razak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universiti_Tun_Abdul_Razak) (UNIRAZAK) has set up their regional centre in Kota Kinabalu.[^cite_note-414] As of 2016, there is around 15 private colleges, two private university colleges together with other newly established colleges.[^cite_note-415] In 1960, the overall literacy rate in North Borneo was only 24%.[^cite_note-416] The recent findings in 2011 found the literacy rate have increase to 79%.[^cite_note-417] Most of secondary schools leavers also did not continue their studies after completing their [Sijil Pelajaran Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sijil_Pelajaran_Malaysia) (SPM) mainly due to financial burdens as well as a lack of interest and confidence to continue their studies in local higher learning institutes. A 2015 survey found that only 16,000 out of more than 20,000 secondary schools leavers continued their studies.[^cite_note-418]



Sabahan secondary school students in their [uniform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_uniforms_by_country#Malaysia)

In early 2016, Sabah had a total number of 42,047 teachers teaching in various pre-schools, primary and secondary schools.[^cite_note-419] Following the decentralisation of power from the federal government to state government as well to improve the education in the state, there has been a target to recruit 90% of teachers from Sabah itself.[^cite_note-420] The [Sabah State Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_State_Library) is the main public library in the state.[^cite_note-421] There are 11 Indonesian schools (beside the main Indonesian school in the state capital) spreading across Sabah mainly for Indonesian migrants children residing in the state.[^cite_note-422] Since 2014, Filipino migrants children also have been enrolled in Alternative Learning Centres (ALC) that were set up by [Filipino volunteers in Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipinos_in_Malaysia) in collaboration with various local non-governmental organisations (NGO).[^cite_note-423]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Sabah)

### Ethnicity and immigration

| Ethnic |  | Percent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other [Bumiputera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bumiputera_\(Malaysia\)) |  | 19.69% |
| [Kadazan-Dusun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kadazan-Dusun) |  | 19.33% |
| [Bajau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bajau_people) |  | 16.54% |
| [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese) |  | 7.28% |
| [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malays_\(ethnic_group\)) |  | 8.99% |
| [Murut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murut_people) |  | 3.11% |
| Other local non-native |  | 1.34% |
| Non-Malaysian citizen |  | 23.71% |

According to the 2020 Malaysian census, the population of Sabah stands at 3,418,785, making Sabah the third most populous state in Malaysia with the highest non-citizen population at 810,443.[^cite_note-OwnCloud::DOSM-424] However, as Malaysia is one of the [least densely populated countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_density) in Asia, Sabah is particularly sparsely populated with most of the population concentrated in the coastal areas since towns and urban centres have massively expanded. People from Sabah are generally called Sabahans and identify themselves as such.[^cite_note-425] There are an estimated 42 ethnic groups with over 200 [sub-ethnic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-ethnic_groups) with separate own languages, cultures and belief systems.[^cite_note-426] The three largest indigenous groups in Sabah are the [Kadazan-Dusun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kadazan-Dusun), [Bajau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bajau_people) and the [Murut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murut_people). There are large [Rungus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rungus_people), [Orang Sungei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orang_Sungai), [Bruneian Malays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruneian_Malays), [Lundayeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lun_Bawang), [Suluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suluk_people) and other [Bumiputera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bumiputera_\(Malaysia\)) ethnic minorities,[^cite_note-indigenous_culture-427] while the [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese) makes up the main non-indigenous population.[^cite_note-sbh-2] High migration to the state was noticeable in the 1970s, when hundreds of thousands of [Filipino refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_Philippines), mostly the [Moros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moro_people), began arriving due to the [Moro conflict in the county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moro_conflict_in_the_Philippines). There are also [Indonesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesians_in_Malaysia) labourers from Kalimantan, [Sulawesi](28740.md) and [Lesser Sunda Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesser_Sunda_Islands).[^cite_note-428][^cite_note-429] The arrival of uncontrollable illegal immigration have cause major impact on political, economic and socio-cultural problem particularly on local indigenous peoples. The arrival of these illegal immigrants and the slow economic growth have forced Sabahans to immigrate to Peninsular Malaysia or overseas to find better paying jobs and income opportunities.[^cite_note-GDP-273][^cite_note-430][^cite_note-431]

### Religion

| Religion |  | Percent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Islam](6037917.md) |  | 69.6% |
| [Christianity](5211.md) |  | 24.7% |
| [Buddhism](3267529.md) |  | 5.1% |
| [No religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) |  | 0.4% |
| Others |  | 0.1% |
| [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) |  | 0.1% |

[Islam](6037917.md) is the predominant religion in Sabah, although its society remained [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism).[^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434] In the 2020 census, the percentage of Muslims was around 69.6%, while that of [Christians](5211.md) was 24.7% and that of [Buddhism](3267529.md) 5.1%.[^cite_note-Census_2020-432] In 1960, the population percentage of Muslims was only 37.9%, while the percentage of Christians was 16.6% and almost half of the population professed other religions, that is at 45.5%.[^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436][^cite_note-437] The increase was mainly contributed through the uncontrolled high immigration rate and controversial mass conversions in recent decades.[^cite_note-controversial-438][^cite_note-Hui2013-439]

The *Colony of North Borneo Annual Report, 1960*, notes that major indigenous groups maintained pagan beliefs, while coastal areas were majority Muslim. Other religions mentioned were traditional Chinese beliefs and various Christian denominations.[^cite_note-440] Many indigenous groups saw conversions to Christianity,[^cite_note-441] in part due to cultural compatibility such as having no ban on pork.[^cite_note-442]

Several other religions, such as [Chinese folk religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_folk_religion) as well as the Indian religions of [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) and [Sikhism](27964.md), are also practised in the state.[^cite_note-443]

### Languages

[Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language) is the main language spoken in the state, although with a different [creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Malay) from [Sarawak Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarawak_Malay) and Peninsular Malay.[^cite_note-444] The state has its own local vernacular for Malay which originated either from indigenous words, [Brunei Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei_Malay), [Bajau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sama%E2%80%93Bajaw_languages), [Suluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suluk_language) and [Dusun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dusun_language) languages.[^cite_note-445] Most of the indigenous languages of Sabah belong to one of the following language families: [Dusunic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dusunic_languages), [Paitanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paitanic_languages), Bisaya–[Lotud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lotud_language), [Murutic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murutic_languages), [Papar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papar_language), [Tatanaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatana_language), [Idaʼan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ida%CA%BCan_language), [Bonggi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonggi_language), all belonging to the larger grouping of [Sabahan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabahan_languages); [Palawanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palawanic_languages) (the [Molbog language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molbog_language)), and [Sama–Bajau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sama%E2%80%93Bajaw_languages) languages from the [Barito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barito_languages) branch.[^cite_note-446] Several indigenous languages are endangered due to the widespread use of Malay particularly at home, as parents often see little use for the indigenous languages in formal environments, such as at work. However, as awareness about the value of indigenous languages is spreading, more parents insist on passing down their mother tongue.[^cite_note-447][^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449] As the [Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka) form a majority of the [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese) in Sabah, the [Hakka languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_languages) (among others, varieties of [Moiyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meixian_dialect) and [Hoiliuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hailu_Hakka)) are the most commonly spoken [Sinitic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinitic_languages) in the state. Other Sinitic languages spoken include several varieties of the [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien) and [Teochew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teochew_Min) languages, [Hainanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainanese) and other [Banlam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Min) languages, [Hinghua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pu-Xian_Min) varieties, [Eastern Min](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Min) languages (e.g. [Hok-chiu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhounese)); all belonging to the larger grouping of [Min languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Min_languages). [Yue languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yue_languages) such as [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese) and [Hoisanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoisanese) are also spoken. [Jilu Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jilu_Mandarin) and [Jiaoliao Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiaoliao_Mandarin) are spoken natively by the so called Huáběi-ren of Sabah, in addition to the standard [Malaysian Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Mandarin) used as a lingua franca between different [Malaysian Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese) groups, speaking mutually unintelligible languages.[^cite_note-450] [Semporna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semporna) is home to the only [Chabacano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chabacano)-speaking community in Malaysia. Semporna's Chabacano speakers are refugees (or descendants of refugees) who fled the [Moro conflict](4602307.md) of the [Philippines](23440.md). A large number of these refugees live in Malaysia illegally. Chabacano is a co-official language there.[^cite_note-451]

&#x20;\
&#x20;Following the change of the government after the 2018 general election, the new Sabah government has stated that there is no restriction on the usage of [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) in the state, adding that even if the Education Ministry stated that it is unlawful for English be used in Sabah, the restrictions will not be allowed to be imposed in the state, and that the state government will undo the previous improper law since the restrictions will only cause more damage to their younger generations especially when they need to work in private firms or organisations that require English proficiency. The new state government also stated that they will look into the matters if there is a need for a change in the state law.[^cite_note-452]

## Culture

See also: [Culture of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Malaysia)



The branch building of [National Department for Culture and Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Department_for_Culture_and_Arts) in Kota Kinabalu

Sabah culture is diverse due to a wide range of different ethnicity.[^cite_note-indigenous_culture-427] In the coastal areas, Sabahan culture has been influenced by the Bruneian Malays and West Coast Bajaus on the west coast side while in the east coast it is influenced by either East Coast Bajau, Bugis, and Suluk cultures with Islam being the important part of their lives.[^cite_note-culture_relationship-453][^cite_note-454] Christianity plays an important part to the indigenous cultures in the interior side in the daily lives of the Kadazan-Dusun, Lundayeh, Murut and Rungus beside their old practice of the traditional Animism and Paganism.[^cite_note-culture_relationship-453] The indigenous culture however are in danger and facing extinction due to widespread cultural assimilation from Peninsular to the state. Controversial [Malayisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayisation) take place in the state since Malaya-based political parties took over the Sabah Government.[^cite_note-455][^cite_note-456]

There is a number of cultural villages exhibiting Sabah indigenous cultures such as the Borneo Cultural Village,[^cite_note-457] [Mari Mari Cultural Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mari_Mari_Cultural_Village),[^cite_note-458] and [Monsopiad Heritage Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monsopiad_Heritage_Village),[^cite_note-459] where cultural performances are also performed. [Sabah Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Museum) houses a number of collection of various [artefacts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artifact_\(archaeology\)), [brassware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brass) and [ceramics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceramics) covering the diverse culture of Sabah, [natural history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_history), [trade history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_international_trade) and [Islamic civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world) together with an ethnobotanical garden and science and technology centre.[^cite_note-460] Other museums include the [Agop Batu Tulug Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agop_Batu_Tulug_Caves), [Agnes Keith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnes_Newton_Keith) House (*[Newlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnes_Keith_House)*), [Pogunon Community Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogunon_Community_Museum), [Sandakan Heritage Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandakan_Heritage_Museum), Teck Guan Cocoa Museum and 3D Wonders Museum.[^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462][^cite_note-463] There is also a number of preserved British, German and Japanese [colonial architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_architecture) such as the [Atkinson Clock Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atkinson_Clock_Tower), [Batu Tinagat Lighthouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batu_Tinagat_Lighthouse), [Jesselton Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesselton_Hotel), ruins of [Kinarut Mansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinarut_Mansion), the [Sabah Tourism Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Tourism_Board) building, [Tawau Bell Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawau_Bell_Tower) together with a number of memorials and monuments. Other unique tourist attractions include the *Rumah Terbalik* (Upside Down House) and Borneo Ant House.[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-465]
